Their harness is this harness: `run_turn(..., max_iters=16)`, no override, the same reply cap. The per-turn record of the 189-call run reads turn one eleven iterations and a real reply, then sixteen iterations and an empty reply on nine of the remaining eleven turns.

**What that changes.** Every sentence above that says the native box never caps is withdrawn, and the two-symptom split of the previous section with it. The poll loop is reachable in containers on native hardware: one container run in three landed on the full signature, 189 calls and nine capped, the same numbers as here. WSL2 is not required. What differs between the boxes is the rate: here the fork's launcher on 0.28 lands there in ten of eleven runs and stock-style arguments in about a third; there, bare runs sit at zero or one capped and containers at one, with one nine in three. Bare is the quietest configuration on the native box and it is not clean.

**What that leaves.** The container layer is implicated for both the wandering and the poll loop; this box's higher rate remains unexplained and is the part a bare-venv run under WSL2 here, or the same hardware under native Linux, would split. The production tier's rows stand: int4 with DFlash2 under the launcher, 0 to 2 capped on both versions here. And the instrument lesson sharpens: the two seats compared "capped" for a day while counting different finishes; the metric's definition belongs beside its first number.

### A root candidate for the scatter, pre-registered (04:45Z, September 4)

The bare venv on the native box is identical to the digit across runs; containers scatter on both boxes. What a fresh container re-decides at every boot and a bare install remembers on disk is kernel selection: Triton's autotune cache and torch.compile's inductor cache live under the user's home. And vLLM, on 0.27.1 and 0.28.0 alike, defaults inductor's `combo_kernels` and `benchmark_combo_kernel` to true when the user leaves them unset (`config/compilation.py`), which means the kernel used for a fused op is chosen by timing it at compile. Timing is not repeatable; a different winner is a different reduction order; a different reduction order is a different greedy trajectory. Every container cell in this document also ran with `VLLM_DISABLE_COMPILE_CACHE=1`, and the stock-style cells had no persisted home, so this box re-timed at every boot by construction.

Predictions, written before the rows: (a) the fork's launcher with the compile cache enabled and the shared cache volume mounted, two boots, lands on identical tool-call totals; (b) with combo-kernel benchmarking off, three boots land on identical totals whether or not the cache is enabled; (c) if the poll loop is a property of some kernel winners and not others, the benchmarking-off arm's capped count is stable across boots, at whatever value it settles on; if the poll loop is independent of kernel choice, it keeps scattering. Seven cells are running here; the native box runs the bare and container halves with caches wiped and persisted.

### The rows against the predictions (05:20Z, September 4)

| arm, this box, fork launcher, bf16 spec-off | boots | tool calls | capped |
|---|---|---|---|
| compile cache enabled, shared cache volume | 2 | 144, 144 | 7, 7 |
| combo-kernel benchmarking off, cache still disabled | 3 | 144, 172, 144 | 7, 9, 7 |
| both (benchmarking off, cache enabled) | 2 | 67, 67 | 1, 1 |

Prediction (a) holds twice: with the cache persisted, two boots are identical, and the two pairs show the mechanism whole. The cache-on pair's compilation config already had a cache entry and both boots reused a bad draw, 144 and 7; the "both" pair's config had none, its first boot ran a fresh race and drew a good winner, 67 and 1, and its second boot reused it exactly. A persisted cache freezes whichever draw the first boot made; without one, every boot draws again. Prediction (b) fails: with the benchmarking knob off and the cache still disabled, boots still differ. So the per-boot variable is not the combo-kernel benchmark; it is what the cache pins. vLLM redirects both inductor's cache and Triton's cache into its own cache directory (`compilation/compiler_interface.py`, the block that sets `TORCHINDUCTOR_CACHE_DIR` and `TRITON_CACHE_DIR`) and turns on Triton's autotune-result caching by default with the comment "without this, Triton re-runs autotuning on every process restart" (`env_override.py`). Triton's autotuner picks a kernel configuration by timing candidates, on purpose, for speed; the result is written to disk so the next boot reuses it. `VLLM_DISABLE_COMPILE_CACHE=1`, which every container cell in this document set, and a fresh container with no persisted home, both make every boot re-run that race. A different winner is a different tiling and reduction order, a slightly different float result, a different argmax on a near tie, a different greedy trajectory, and on this task a different chance of the poll loop. The native box's arm A shows the other half: within one boot, two missions are identical in all 124 calls; four boots of the same image were four different fixed points.

What this does and does not say. It says the scatter is per-boot kernel selection re-raced when the caches are absent, and that persisting vLLM's cache directory makes a container as deterministic as a bare install. It does not say which winners cause the poll loop or why this box's rate is higher; it does say the earlier environment reasoning (drivers, WSL2 alone) was chasing a consequence of the harness's own cache flag and of fresh containers. For the fork's users: mount a persistent `~/.cache/vllm` (or the launcher's `/cache`) into the container and leave `VLLM_DISABLE_COMPILE_CACHE` unset, and A/B rows become comparable across boots.

### What the race chooses, and the pin cycle, pre-registered (05:40Z, September 4)

The persisted cache records every candidate's timing per kernel in `*.autotune.json`, so the two frozen draws can be compared choice by choice. Six kernels are autotuned on this cell, all of them the chunked Gated DeltaNet kernels of the model's linear-attention layers (`third_party/flash_linear_attention/ops/`), the same layers batch-invariant mode refuses. Three chose the same configuration in both draws. Three did not:

| kernel | good draw (67 calls, 1 capped) | bad draw (144 calls, 7 capped) | candidates |
|---|---|---|---|
| `chunk_scaled_dot_kkt_fwd_kernel` | BK=32, 4 warps, 4 stages, 0.0078 ms | BK=64, 8 warps, 3 stages, 0.0076 ms | 27 |
| `merge_16x16_to_64x64_inverse_kernel` | 2 warps, 2 stages | 4 warps, 2 stages | 12 |
| `recompute_w_u_fwd_kernel` | 4 warps, 4 stages | 8 warps, 4 stages | 9 |

The winners are within three percent of each other in timing, which is why any jitter flips them. The mathematics is the same formula; the block size and warp count change the order in which the dot products of the delta rule's K·Kᵀ, the block inverse and the recomputed W and U are accumulated, bfloat16 and fp32 round differently in the last bits, the linear-attention state after prefill differs slightly, and a near-tied argmax somewhere in twelve turns lands on the other side.

A persisted cache is a pin: a boot with the cache uses the recorded winner. The cycle, one boot per cell, predictions written first: on the good tree, force each differing kernel in turn to the bad draw's choice; on the bad tree, force each to the good draw's choice. If one kernel carries the trajectory, its forced cell flips (67/1 to a capped run, and 144/7 to a clean one) while the other two stay put; if the effect is the sum of small perturbations, any forced kernel moves the trajectory somewhere new and no single one restores the other draw. The K·Kᵀ kernel is the prior favourite: it is the largest reduction of the three and its block size is what changed.

### The pin cycle: one kernel, one warp count (05:50Z, September 4)

| tree | kernel forced | boot result |
|---|---|---|
| good (67 calls, 1 capped) | K·Kᵀ to the bad draw's choice (BK=64, 8 warps, 3 stages) | 67 / 1, unchanged |
| good | **block-inverse merge, 2 warps to 4** | **144 / 7, the bad draw to the digit** |
| good | recompute-W-U to the bad draw's choice (8 warps) | 67 / 1, unchanged |
| bad (144 calls, 7 capped) | K·Kᵀ to the good draw's choice | 144 / 7, unchanged |
| bad | **block-inverse merge, 4 warps to 2** | **126 / 5, a third fixed point** |
| bad | recompute-W-U to the good draw's choice | 144 / 7, unchanged |

The pins were verified live: the forced timings were still in the record after the boot and the file's modification time was the pin's, so Triton read the cache and did not re-race. ~~Two of the three differing kernels are inert on this cell.~~ **Struck 15:10Z, September 4, by the draw test below: they are inert in the two trees the cycle froze, not in general. Draws 1 and 2 carry the same merge configuration (2 warps, 2 stages) and differ only in those two supposedly inert kernels, and they land on different trajectories (160 calls with 7 capped against 188 with 9). The cycle's finding survives only in its narrow form: within a fixed set of other winners, the merge kernel's warp count moves the trajectory. It is not the sole decider.**  The third carries it: `merge_16x16_to_64x64_inverse_kernel` in `third_party/flash_linear_attention/ops/solve_tril.py`, the kernel that merges four 16x16 lower-triangular block inverses into a 64x64 inverse for the chunked delta rule, autotuned over three warp counts and four stage counts on a key of heads, chunk size and variable-length flag, and written out with a round-to-nearest-even downcast from fp32 to bf16. Two warps against four is a different partition of the same tiles, so the fp32 accumulation of the block products happens in a different order, the downcast rounds a few elements the other way, the linear-attention state after prefill differs in its last bits, and the model's twelve-turn conversation lands on the other side of a near tie. From the good tree that one change reproduces the bad draw exactly; from the bad tree the reverse lands on a third trajectory, because the bad tree also carries the inductor's fusions under benchmarking on and the other two winners, so the set as a whole decides where the trajectory can fall and this one choice decides which side. The two configurations timed 0.0147 and 0.0143 milliseconds, three percent apart.

**What follows for the fork's users.** Rows from different boots are comparable only with the cache persisted, and a persisted cache is one frozen draw; the write-up's own capping distributions are distributions over draws. Recording the autotune winners beside a row (the `*.autotune.json` files under the cache directory) would make a draw reproducible on another box. For the kernel itself, the fastest and the second-fastest warp count differ by three percent in time and by the whole trajectory in effect, so pinning `num_warps` for that kernel, or a batch-invariant path for the linear-attention kernels, is the change that would remove the race rather than freeze it; that is an upstream question, offered on the PR thread with the table.

### The other box's determinism cut, and what capping actually is (07:00Z, September 4)

Four arms on the native 3090, each repeated, closing the container question:

| arm | configuration | result |
|---|---|---|
| A | container, one boot, two missions | 124 calls / 4 capped, identical call for call |
| B | container, cache persisted, two boots | 58 / 0, identical, wall time within 0.1 s |
| C | bare, both caches deleted before each run | 160 / 7, identical twice |
| E | container, combo kernels and their benchmarking off, no persisted cache | 160 / 7, then 77 / 1, then 160 / 7 |
| ref | bare, cache intact | 86 / 1, four times |
| ref | container, no persisted cache | 124 / 4, 189 / 9, 78 / 1, 74 / 1 |

Arm C is the one that corrects our reading. Deleting the cache on a bare box does not scatter: it moves the fixed point and keeps it fixed. So the sentence is not "no cache, therefore scatter." It is that a missing cache forces the timing race to be re-run, and the race is only as reproducible as the box's timing is; a quiet bare box re-times to the same winners every boot, and a container's noise does not. This box disables the compile cache on every campaign cell and runs in a container, so it re-times under noise on every boot, which is the worst of the two by construction. Arm E says turning the benchmarking off is not sufficient, so persisting the cache is the only lever of the three that worked, and it buys repeatability rather than a good trajectory: their persisted arm froze on 0 capped, their bare-wiped arm froze on 7, and both are stable.

**Capping is not a separate defect.** Their rows put capped turns in step with tool-call count, and ours agree on 135 twelve-turn runs across every image and cell in this campaign: the correlation is 0.964, and there is no run above 140 calls with one capped turn or below 80 calls with four. The arithmetic explains part of it, since a capped turn contributes sixteen calls by definition, but not the substance: the turns that finish normally also lengthen with it.

| capped turns | runs | mean tool calls | mean iterations in the turns that finished |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 41 | 43.1 | 3.72 |
| 1 | 44 | 73.8 | 4.91 |
| 2 | 5 | 85.2 | 4.06 |
| 3 | 5 | 99.6 | 5.42 |
| 5 | 1 | 134.0 | 6.29 |
| 7 | 23 | 160.4 | 6.89 |
| 9 | 16 | 188.2 | 9.00 |

So one variable moves: how long the model polls before it commits to an answer. Capping is that variable's tail against a fixed iteration budget, not a distinct failure mode, and the write-up's capping counts should be read as a coarse measure of loop length. The attractors are also shared between the two boxes rather than per-machine: 160/7, 189/9, 124/4, 86/1, 77-78/1 and 58/0 appear on a 3090 and on these 4090s alike, which fits a small set of trajectories selected at boot rather than a hardware-specific defect. What differs between the boxes is the odds of landing on each, and that is what the native-Linux cut on this hardware would separate.

### Bug or arithmetic: the merge kernel is equally correct either way (13:40Z, September 4)

The pin cycle said the block-inverse merge kernel's warp count decides the trajectory. It did not say whether one of the two is wrong. Running the kernel at both warp counts on identical inputs, autotune bypassed, against a float64 reference computed on the same values:

| input scale | 2 warps, relative error | 4 warps, relative error | elements that disagree |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.05 | 2.684e-08 | 2.684e-08 | 2.5% |
| 0.25 | 1.268e-07 | 1.262e-07 | 19.5% |
| 1.00 | 2.600e-07 | 4.425e-07 | 30.6% |

Neither configuration is nearer the truth. Both sit at the error a bf16 input and an fp32 accumulation earn, and they disagree with each other only in the last bits. So there is no incorrect kernel here to fix. What is real is that greedy decoding turns a last-bit disagreement into a different path when the model is near a tie on this scenario, and that a benchmark row is not reproducible without its compile-cache state. The instrument is `results/raw/v028/.kernel_accuracy.py` in the bench repo. At input scale 1.0 the matrix being inverted is ill conditioned and both configurations lose absolute accuracy together, which is a property of the problem rather than of either kernel.

**The pin cycle was two frozen trees.** It shows the kernel is decisive between those two draws; it does not show its warp count predicts the trajectory across independent draws. Eight fresh draws are running with the prediction registered first: two warps gives the short loop, four gives the long one. Each wipes the compile cache so the race re-runs and leaves it enabled so the winner is recorded.

### What was filed, and the census on the acceptance regression (13:55Z)

Two issues opened at the maintainer's request. The first retracts this document's own capping item, which he had entered in the tracker as a live bug in main: the cap is this bench's per-turn budget rather than anything the serving stack imposes, it is not specific to 0.28, and the claim that turning prefix caching off removes it does not survive its own rows (74/1, 70/1, 25/0 and 106/3). The second is the autotune nondeterminism and the compile-cache rule, so the rule he drew from it has somewhere to point.

The third item is his, and it is the one open ask: on the reference 3090 the merged 0.28 accepts 3.19 tokens per verify step against 0.27.1's 2.66 with DFlash2, which is the same regression this document measured independently at 3.1 against 2.6. He asked for a bisect of the DFlash2 patch group rather than a guess at the 4,247 changed lines of the lookup patch. The census answers that without a boot: the group is unchanged hunk for hunk except in one file, where the speculator patch goes from 10 hunks to 15 and the model runner from 2 to 3. Three of the five new hunks change behaviour.

1. The drafter is asked for fewer tokens per step. The 0.28 form clamps `draft_block` to the checkpoint's trained block when the lookup is on, and `num_query_per_req` follows it, where 0.27.1 used the full speculative width. The remaining verify positions are filled from context instead of from the drafter, and the patch's own comment names `VLLM_DFLASH2_LOOKUP=0` as the honest control.
2. The selector walk lost its float64 path. The cast that read `tl.float64 if USE_FP64 else tl.float32` is now `tl.float32`, while the flag stays declared and passed, so the knob looks live and no longer selects that cast.
3. The proposal distribution is truncated to the request's top-k and top-p, disabled by `VLLM_DFLASH2_DRAFT_TOPK_TOPP=0`, and it cannot explain a loss at temperature 0 because it never runs there.

Incidental from the same census: three patches on merged main carry hunks against `.orig` files, which are the backup files `patch` writes, so the series was regenerated from a tree that still had them. Inert at runtime, and the fingerprint of the regeneration that dropped the page-padding hunk in the first place.

### The draw test, and the correction it forced (15:10Z, September 4)

The pin cycle froze two trees and forced six pins, which is n=2 on the thing that matters. Eight fresh draws are running with the prediction registered in advance: two warps on the block-inverse merge kernel gives the short loop, four gives the long one. The first two draws answer it.

| draw | merge kernel | K-transpose-K kernel | recompute-W-U kernel | trajectory |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2 warps, 2 stages | 8 warps, 3 stages, BK 64 | 8 warps, 3 stages | 160 calls, 7 capped |
| 2 | 2 warps, 2 stages | 4 warps, 4 stages, BK 32 | 4 warps, 4 stages | 188 calls, 9 capped |

The prediction is wrong twice over. Two warps gave the long loop both times, and the same merge configuration gave two different trajectories. The kernels that moved are the two the cycle reported as inert, so their inertness was a property of the trees the cycle froze rather than of the kernels. The honest claim is the weaker one: the winner set as a whole selects the trajectory, and any member of it can move the result. The merge kernel is decisive between the two particular draws the cycle compared, which is a real finding about those draws and not a general one.

This is the value of registering a prediction and then drawing rather than pinning. The strong claim went out on the pull request thread and in the autotune issue, and it needs correcting there once the remaining draws are in.

### The acceptance regression (#73): three mechanisms proposed, three retracted (15:30Z, September 4)

The maintainer asked for a bisect of the DFlash2 patch group rather than a guess at the 4,247 changed lines of the lookup patch. The census answered without a boot and answered wrongly, three times.

**The method was wrong first.** Comparing the patch files hunk by hunk flags a hunk that merely grew or moved its boundaries as new behaviour. The right comparison is the installed files. Pulling both speculators out of the two built images gives 86 changed lines in one file, and says that `dflash2/lookup.py` is byte-identical between the trees, which the patch-level reading never revealed.

**Then the conditions retired what was left.** The draft-block clamp turned out to be present in the 0.27.1 tree too, at the same lines, which a 0.27.1 boot confirms by logging the same clamped draft width. And every remaining difference is a division by temperature: the cached proposal row, the truncation site, and a greedy-only tie-break change. The checkpoint's configuration sets temperature 1.0, the maintainer's decode guard passes no temperature flag, and a boot here logs the server resolving to 1.0. At exactly 1.0, dividing by temperature is the identity, so none of them can act in the regime the number was measured in.

**The control, run rather than argued.** The one-line restoration of the temperature-scaled proposal row, against an unedited arm, one frozen kernel draw, the maintainer's own decode guard:

| arm | tokens per step |
|---|---|
| as merged | 2.78 |
| proposal row restored | 2.77 |

Identical, as the arithmetic requires. The same pair also shows why this box cannot publish absolute throughput: prefill moved from 3,303 to 705 tokens per second between the two arms, and the edit sits in the draft selector where it cannot touch prefill at all, so that difference is the box rather than the change. The ratio within a run survived what the absolute numbers did not.

**What survives, and where it points.** The premise the 0.28 code states twice, at the caching site and again in the draft-logits spec, is that the rejection sampler divides cached draft logits by temperature itself. An independent read of the installed sampler on the other box found it applies temperature to the target side only and leaves the draft side untouched. That inconsistency is real and inert at temperature 0 or 1, and would bite at any other value.

The open question is now a metric question rather than a performance one. Tokens per step is derived as one plus accepted over drafts from two engine counters, and the two arms are different engine versions, so the ruler is part of the delta rather than a fixed measure across it. At the tested width the drafter proposes seven tokens while the lookup fills eight. The check needs no cross-tree comparison at all: drafted tokens divided by drafts, within a single arm, says whether the lookup-filled positions are counted as drafts.

One instrument result worth carrying. The teacher-forced acceptance bench keys its cached target on the prompt alone, with no build, width, or boot in the key, so replaying one configuration's target under another is expected to go off trajectory whenever the batch shape changes, which the bench's own header documents. Its clean-chunk fraction is the validity guard, and a tokens-per-step figure computed over a handful of surviving chunks is not a measurement. That instrument is invalid across builds whose text diverges; the maintainer's own decode guard is not, because there each build generates its own continuation and scores its own drafter against its own target.

### The acceptance regression, found: an unset launcher field (16:10Z, September 4)

The fourth mechanism is the one with a confirming arm, and the evidence that matters needs no patch at all.

**The config fact.** On 0.27.1 the fork's own speculator allocated the draft-logits buffer unconditionally, with a comment stating why: its selector samples probabilistically for non-greedy requests, so rejection sampling always needs the realized proposal distribution. The 0.28 port dropped that unconditional allocation and inherited the upstream base class, which allocates only when the speculative config asks for probabilistic draft sampling. Upstream's default is greedy, documented as treating the draft probabilities as one-hot. The launcher sets that field explicitly for the other speculator and omits it for this one, so the merged tree silently runs the other behaviour. Both boot logs say so outright, reporting the buffer present on one tree and absent on the other.

**The mechanism.** With no proposal distribution the rejection kernel treats the draft probability as one, and the acceptance test collapses from the ratio of target to draft down to the bare target probability. Since the draft probability is at most one, the ratio test accepts at least as often, so the merged path is strictly stricter. That predicts the direction, which the first reading of the change did not.

**The control that needs no edit.** Running the sibling speculator over the same two builds, same box, same hour, same workload:

| path | launcher sets the field | acceptance change across the two builds |
|---|---|---|
| the other speculator | yes | plus 6.5% |
| this one | no | minus 10.5% |

If the port carried a general acceptance regression, both would show it.

**The counters, three boxes.** Round size is unchanged everywhere, so this is proposal survival rather than block length.

| box | 0.27.1 | merged | change |
|---|---|---|---|
| maintainer's, tokens per step | 3.19 | 2.66 | minus 16.6% |
| this box, tokens per step | 3.176 | 2.621 | minus 17.5% |
| native 3090, tokens per step | 3.751 | 3.457 | minus 7.8% |

**The fix arm.** Setting the field recovers most or all of the gap. On the native box acceptance per drafted token goes from 34.4% to 36.8% against a 38.4% reference. On this box two runs give 32.1% and 30.2% against a 30.3% reference, so the recovery reaches parity rather than exceeding it; the first run alone suggested an overshoot and the repeat retired that reading.

**What the repeats mean, and they mean different things per box.** The native box seeds from zero and holds its kernels fixed, so its repeats came back bit identical on every counter and are replays rather than samples. This box holds the seed too but re-races the autotuner every boot, so its repeats vary the kernel axis: two shipped runs agree within one percent relative, while two runs with the field set differ by 5.6%. That spread difference is itself consistent with the mechanism, since the shipped path selects proposals deterministically and the fixed path samples them. So the kernel axis is bounded here and the seed axis is bounded nowhere yet, which is what the other box's seed sweep is for.

**The residual, stated plainly.** The field does not explain everything on the native box, and the untested candidate for the remainder is the emitted-token accounting patch the maintainer bisected himself as worth about a quarter of the gap, which feeds the block-length policy and is the natural explanation for the small round-size drift on the fixed arm.

**A caution worth carrying.** Five separate candidates in this component were retired by the same argument: nearly every difference in it is a question of where a division by temperature happens, and the measured regime runs at exactly one, where that division is the identity in every floating-point format. A diff cannot tell you whether a change can act in the regime the number came from.

### The cohort result, stratified: the fix works only where the drafter carries the block (16:30Z, September 4)

Four seeds by eight prompts at 1024 output tokens, paired by seed and prompt, tokens per step as the primary metric, split on the shipped block size so the stratifier is a pre-treatment variable. The threshold was registered before the data.

| stratum | cells | shipped | field set | t |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| overall | 32 | 3.440 | 3.730 | 2.77 |
| drafted tokens per round = 7 | 18 | 2.886 | 3.425 | 4.22 |
| drafted tokens per round > 7 | 14 | 4.152 | 4.123 | -0.22 |

So the overall effect is suggestive rather than established, and the stratification is the finding. Positions filled from context never came from the drafter, so they carry no proposal distribution to normalise against in either configuration, and the field's effect dilutes to nothing where the lookup is engaged. That reading was registered in advance as the interesting outcome rather than found afterwards, and the same direction appears on the other box at a quarter of the sample size.

The earlier short-prompt figure of plus 0.48 tokens per step is therefore a stratum-specific result, since every row of that cell had the lookup inert. The overall figure is also a mixture whose value depends on what fraction of a workload sits in each stratum, so it is a property of the cohort rather than a constant of the fix.

Two ordering constraints came out of the other box's replication. The degeneracy guard must run before any stratification on block size, because a collapsed generation makes the lookup engage and therefore sorts into the engaged stratum by construction, where a single such row moved their stratum estimate by two tokens per step and flipped their overall sign. And per-request figures are comparable only within a fixed request order, because the long block is sticky and coasts on prior state without consulting the emitted-token count.

The report drafted for the issue is at `docs/drafts/issue-73-report.draft.md`.

### What the pieces are, for the reader who wants the mechanism and not only the numbers (September 5)

**The six autotuned kernels.** All of them belong to the chunked Gated DeltaNet path, the linear-attention layers this model uses in place of most of its softmax attention. Each processes the sequence in chunks of 64 tokens, and the recurrent state is carried between chunks. Triton autotunes a kernel by timing a small grid of launch configurations at first use, warps per block and pipeline stages, and keeping the fastest. `chunk_local_cumsum_scalar` computes the running sum of the gate values inside a chunk. `chunk_scaled_dot_kkt_fwd` forms the key-times-key-transpose product that the delta rule needs. `merge_16x16_to_64x64_inverse` builds the inverse of a 64 by 64 lower-triangular matrix from four 16 by 16 inverses, which is the solve that turns the delta rule into a chunked recurrence. `recompute_w_u_fwd` recomputes two intermediate tensors from that inverse. `chunk_gated_delta_rule_fwd_h` carries the state across chunks. `chunk_fwd_kernel_o` produces the layer output. Two of the six never changed configuration across eight cold boots here, four took three or four different winners each, and no two boots agreed on the full set. A different winner is a different partition of the same tiles, therefore a different order of floating-point accumulation, therefore a different last bit, and both warp counts of the merge kernel sit at the same distance from a float64 reference, so this is rounding order and not accuracy.

**Why one bit moves a conversation.** At temperature zero the next token is the argmax. Two candidates that the model ranks within a rounding error of each other are decided by that last bit, and once the conversation takes the other branch it never rejoins. The twelve-turn tool loop has such a near tie early, which is why one image gave 58 to 189 tool calls across boots. At the model's recommended sampling the choice at that position is a draw from a distribution rather than a comparison of two nearly equal numbers, so the same last-bit difference changes the odds of a branch by a negligible amount instead of deciding it.

**The field.** `draft_sample_method` tells the speculator whether the drafter picks each proposed token by argmax or by sampling, and, downstream of that, whether the rejection sampler receives the drafter's probability for each proposal. With the field unset the base class allocates no buffer for those probabilities, the selector is handed temperature zero and picks by argmax, and the rejection kernel treats every draft probability as one. The acceptance test in that kernel accepts a proposal when a uniform draw falls under the target probability divided by the draft probability. With the draft probability pinned to one that ratio can only be smaller, so fewer proposals survive. The 0.27.1 fork allocated the buffer unconditionally, which is why the field's absence cost nothing there and cost ten to seventeen percent of tokens per step on 0.28.

**The block-length policy.** With the lookup on, the drafter is asked for the block the checkpoint was trained for, seven tokens, and the engine verifies eight positions. The remaining positions up to the configured width of fifteen are filled from the request's own context by a suffix lookup, but only while a request is reproducing its context, because a long block costs attention on every step whether or not the tail is accepted. The policy that decides this runs on the host once per step, one step stale by design so that it never synchronises the device. It enters the long block only after two qualifying steps in a row, where a qualifying step is one where the lookup had something to put in the tail and the step that just finished emitted a full block. Once entered, `VLLM_DFLASH2_LOOKUP_STICKY` steps of coasting follow, default three, during which the policy consults nothing. That coast is what makes per-request drafted-token counts depend on the previous request.

**The emitted count and the contradicting comment.** The qualifying test reads `last_num_emitted`, which the speculator writes from the sampler's counters on every step. `input_batch.py` defines the rejected count as the number of logits minus the number sampled, on both engines, so the sampled count was always the number of tokens the sampler emitted. The 0.27.1 formula subtracted the rejected count from it and therefore removed every rejection twice. The 0.28 patch corrected the formula; the comment inherited from 0.27.1 described the old meaning and was left standing. Reversing the patch feeds the policy a count that is too low, so it qualifies less often and spends less time in the long block. On the maintainer's cohort that scored higher. Whether that is because the long block was not paying for itself there is exactly what the sticky arm below measures with an honest knob instead of a wrong number.

### The policy knob, measured: nothing left to recover on the production configuration (September 5)

The pull request body claimed the maintainer's 0.13 tokens per step from reversing the emitted-count patch was long-block occupancy reached through a wrong number, and that the policy's own knob would reproduce it honestly. Three arms on the cohort, field set in all three, paired, guard applied first and removing nothing:

| arm | tokens per step | drafted tokens per round |
|---|---|---|
| shipped patch, coast 3 | 3.735 | 7.140 |
| patch reversed, coast 3 | 3.739 | 7.113 |
| shipped patch, coast 0 | 3.737 | 7.055 |

Both knobs lowered occupancy as predicted and neither moved throughput, t of 0.13 and 0.10 against a registered bar of 3, null inside both strata. So the honest knob does what it says to block occupancy and there is nothing on this configuration for it to recover. The likeliest reason is the regime: his measurement was taken with the field unset, where argmax drafting keeps acceptance lower and the emitted count nearer the policy's threshold, so the two formulas disagree more often. With the field set, more steps clear the threshold under either formula and the difference stops mattering, which matches the one-in-ninety-nine disagreement the other box instrumented. The comment fix stands on the definitions alone; the claim that the knob recovers his gain is withdrawn as measured, on the pull request.

### The order effect, mechanism settled (September 5, 02:45Z)

Three arms on this box, six seeds in two orders each, one boot per arm. With an ordinary prompt no request entered the long block and every counter was order-invariant, which is a check that could not fail rather than a null. With a prompt that makes the model reproduce its context the long block engaged on every seed and request order changed drafts and accepted tokens on four of six seeds, one seed from 31 verify rounds to 112, so on this box the effect moves the trajectory itself. With prefix caching genuinely off, confirmed as `enable_prefix_caching=False` in the server log and by the rows differing from the cache-on arm, the effect persisted on four of six seeds. So the driver is the block-length policy coasting into the next request, and prefix caching is ruled out here.

The first cache-off arm was a no-op: the launcher's flag adds engine arguments only at one, and at zero the engine default for a generative hybrid model resolves prefix caching to on, so both arms ran identically and the bit-identical result was forced. The rerun passed the engine's own off switch and printed its resolved setting before its numbers. That rule now stands for every arm in this document: print the engine's resolved configuration, then the number.

## Arc two: throughput. Registrations before numbers (September 4 to 5)

**Arm 1, draft width 3 to 7, per-position acceptance and wall clock.** Registered: the per-position curve decreases steeply; the wall-clock optimum sits below seven if positions five to seven accept under ten percent. Falsifier: decode rate monotone in width up to seven means shorter buys nothing. Before arm 1 reported, the other box's arm-3 cell supplied the curve on the same cohort at production sampling: 80.1 percent at position zero falling to 19.9 at position six, so no drafter position is under ten percent and the premise is false on acceptance grounds. The wall-clock half is what arm 1 still answers.

**The finding neither box registered.** On that same cell, positions zero to six returned 98.8 percent of all accepted tokens and the eight lookup-filled positions returned 1.2 percent, with a thirty-fold drop between position six and position seven. Two readings the cell cannot separate: the long block is rarely entered so those positions rarely exist, or they exist and accept near zero. The counter is cumulative over rounds in which a position existed, so the raw counts are deflated by the first and the two look identical.

**Arm 2, the cheap-context threshold at 0, 4096, 8192 and 16384, is the discriminator, and it now carries two competing hypotheses.** H1, from the source comment: forcing the long block below the threshold wins, eight percent at the C1 cohort, because extra verify positions are cheap at short context. H2, from the curve: forcing it loses, because positions seven to fourteen accept near zero even when they always exist, so the extra positions cost attention and return nothing, and the loss grows with the threshold. The discriminating number is the per-position accept rate at seven to fourteen under the highest threshold, where the long block is unconditional on this cohort. Under two percent is H2 and the lookup is not earning its width on ordinary text; tens of percent is H1 back in play and the lookup was starved rather than wrong. No sign is predicted. The client records per-position deltas on every row, so the cell needs no change.

**Arm 3, drafter truncation under real sampling, on the other box.** Five cells, field set throughout, the rank half of the mechanism made live by matched cells at top-k eight, sign unpredicted, effect registered under 0.15 tokens per step. Halted after an independent review found that a null with no measured truncation activation is not an experiment, and that a fixed request order does not give equal state when truncation changes the outputs that feed the suffix lookup. Two independent reviews of that design, from different models reading the patch files with line numbers, converged on the same four points and are kept at `docs/reviews/arm3-truncation-astra.md` and `docs/reviews/arm3-truncation-sol.md`: the sign argument was wrong because the mask is built from the drafter's own scores rather than the target's support; a fourth gate exists in the attached request sampling state; the lookup overwrites the treatment on the rows it fills; and a lone top-k cell is uninterpretable without a matched pair. One added the power arithmetic: the earlier cohort's paired spread implies about sixty paired rows to resolve the registered 0.15 tokens per step, where eight rows give a half-width of 0.41. Redesign to be registered before further boots. The rule that came out of it: measure that the mechanism fired, not only that the switch was set.

### A box fact that reframes every card-1 clock in this document (September 6)

`nvidia-smi` reports this box's card 1 negotiating PCIe Gen4 at 4 lanes, with 16 available, while card 0 negotiates 8 at idle. Under an identical configuration card 0 decodes at about 162 tokens per second and card 1 at about 39, and card 1 does so at 99 percent utilisation drawing about 155 watts in the P2 state, which is a GPU waiting on its host rather than computing. With WSL2 pinned memory and prefix caching on, every decode step crosses that link.

So every wall-clock figure this document reports from card 1, including the prefill collapse between the two proposal-row arms and the low counter-cell rates, is a four-lane artefact layered on WSL2 and not a property of the change under test. Counter-based ratios from card 1, tokens per step, acceptance and per-position acceptance, are unaffected and were the ones published. Arm 1 runs entirely on card 0 and its decode rates are trustworthy within the card. Arm 2 runs entirely on card 1: its registered discriminator is counter-based and stands; its decode-rate reading is being repeated on card 0 for the two end cells. The other box remains the only one whose absolute rates compare to the maintainer's.

### Arc two, first results and a retraction on the other box (September 6, later)

**Arm 1, width 5 against width 7, 31 paired cells, card 0.** Tokens per step 3.375 against 3.757, a difference of minus 0.382 at t of minus 5.56; decode rate 141.8 against 159.4 tokens per second at t of minus 5.77. Established, in the direction the registered falsifier named: the drafter's last two positions return 184 and 146 accepted tokens per thousand rounds, and dropping them costs more in tokens per step than two fewer verify positions save in wall clock. The per-position counts at positions zero to four are the same at both widths, so narrowing the block does not change how often the early positions accept, it only removes the tail. Widths 4, 3 and 6 still to come.

**Arm 3 on the other box: the sign mechanism retracted, the consumer verified, a counter bug caught, and a replication that breaks the replacement explanation.** The requester withdrew the claim that renormalisation hurts kept tokens on the reviewers' argument that expected acceptance is the sum over tokens of the smaller of target and proposal probability, so renormalising the proposal over the kept set can only raise every surviving term and the only loss channel is target mass on removed tokens. The flag both reviews had marked unverified is closed with installed-tree line numbers: the rejection sampler computes draft log probabilities with a global log-sum-exp over the vocabulary-wide row, the row is filled with negative infinity by the fork's own override of the draft-logits spec, and the cache kernel erases the previous step's candidates before writing, so the proposal density the sampler reads is renormalised over exactly the kept support. A counter placed between the cache write and the lookup reads the truncation's output before the point-mass overwrite. The requester's own pilot half-width of 0.40 tokens per step at eight rows independently confirms the reviewer's derived 0.41.

A rank-only truncation pair, top-k 8 with top-p 1 and lookup on, one boot each and descriptive only, reproduced the positional shape of the top-p pair: a loss at the first one or two draft positions and a gain concentrated at positions two to six. Under rank truncation exactly eight of sixteen candidates are removed at every position, so the explanation that position zero loses because more candidates are cut there is dead, and the surviving account is removed target mass where drafter and target disagree.

**A path registered as a prediction, to be attacked in the next review round.** The verify step scores positions one to k with the proposed tokens at earlier positions as their input, so truncation changes the prefix on which the target's later logits are computed. Per-position deltas are therefore not independent, a late gain can be the downstream effect of a better-supported early proposal, and at position zero, where the proposal is conditioned on a verified prefix and the drafter is most confident, truncation can only remove a target token from the tail and never rescue one. ~~The prediction: the position-zero loss is largest on prompts where the target's entropy at the verified prefix is highest, and near zero where the target is peaked, testable with one logprobs field per request.~~ **Struck on review, September 6.** A point target on a token the drafter misranks loses everything under truncation at zero entropy, and a point target on a kept token gains at the same entropy, so entropy does not locate target mass relative to the kept set; and one sampled logprob is a surprisal, not an entropy. The claim that truncation cannot help at position zero fell to a counterexample as well: a target equal to the renormalised proposal on the drafter's top eight goes from 0.801 to 1.0 with no prefix change. What survives is the path itself, as an architectural inference the patches do not expose, and the bound that governs any account of the loss: the change in expected acceptance is a retained-support gain minus a removed-overlap loss, each at most the selector mass truncated away, so a small truncated mass at position zero makes any direct effect there small regardless of story. The deciding measurement is a matched-prefix record at position zero of the original proposal, the target on the sixteen candidate ids, and the keep mask, joined by prefix and round.

### A rule for every counter on this engine (September 6, from a crash on the other box)

An activation counter hooked inside the draft pass crashed at CUDA-graph capture because its device sync is forbidden there. Had it been graph-safe it would have executed once, at capture time, written a single row, and printed a plausible per-step histogram, because the draft pass is replayed from a captured graph and anything Python does inside it runs at capture time only, as the source itself says. The crash was the honest outcome and the only reason anyone knew.

**An instrument placed inside a replayed region measures the replay, not the run.** Before trusting a counter, ask what executes it and how often. Prefer an instrument that fails loudly to one that degrades quietly. The activation cells now run eager, which is required for the measurement to exist at all rather than merely convenient, and their timing was already declared non-reportable so nothing is lost. The counters this document relies on from the engine's metrics endpoint are accumulated on the host from sampler outputs after each step, outside any captured region, so they measure the run; that had not been asked until this failure asked it.

The smoke test for the mechanistic configuration also self-confirmed in the right way: with the lookup off, the per-position counter reported positions zero to six and nothing above, so the measurement's own geometry proved the setting rather than a log line being trusted.

### What the state-of-the-art sweep found, verified against the primaries (September 6)

The research brief at `docs/research/spec-decode-sota-2026-09.md` is 954 lines with 49 sources and twelve explicit unverified markers. Three of its findings were checked against the merged pull requests and the built 0.28.0 tree before being repeated here.

**The field set for issue 73 is faster and, on 0.28.0, not lossless.** Upstream 54282, merged three days after the tag, decouples the draft's Gumbel noise stream from the target's. Under probabilistic drafting the noise used to re-sample a rejected draft is drawn from the same Philox offset as the noise that produced it, so the residual under-weights exactly the high-probability tokens that lost the draft argmax and the output distribution is no longer the target's. The salt that fixes it is absent from the built tree. Greedy drafting never samples and is unaffected. So arm 3 on the other box measures truncation inside that bias, and production now runs it. The clean fix is a cherry-pick; a note for the maintainer is drafted for the pen at `docs/drafts/note-mads-upstream-fixes.draft.md`.

**Two of the fork's three context profiles run the drafter on a wrong attention schedule.** Upstream 54374 shows FlashAttention's ahead-of-time schedule stays on when the drafter is the only FlashAttention consumer, which is whenever the target runs another backend; the fast profile is correct by coincidence, the long and huge profiles are not, and the symptom is acceptance collapsing to one under full CUDA graphs. Every cell in this arc is on the fast profile. The first arc's huge-context validation ran on the bad path.

**Block verification is already in this tree and switched off.** The rejection-sample method accepts the value block at 0.28.0, wired into the V2 rejection sampler that DFlash2 uses: non-greedy only, lossless, never worse than token-by-token, five to eight percent reported. One configuration key. It is the closest thing to a trick everyone is missing that the sweep found, and it is being measured rather than claimed.

**One claim in the brief is falsified by our own data.** It proposes that a flat per-position acceptance of 0.74 reproduces the reported statistics and so the low acceptance is a depth artefact. The measured curve on the production cohort runs from 80 percent at the first position to 20 at the seventh. It is not flat, and the brief's own counter-citation, a decaying per-position acceptance, is the one that matches.

### Arm 2 decides the cliff, and arm 1 is monotone through three widths (September 6, night)

**Arm 2, first forced cell.** With the cheap-context threshold at 4096 the long block is forced on every round of the cohort, round size exactly 15.000 across all 32 paired cells against 7.172 at the control. Tokens per step 3.769 against 3.739, plus 0.029 at t of 0.58; decode rate minus 0.3 tokens per second at t of minus 0.55, on the four-lane card where the wall clock is host-bound and the cost of extra attention may be hidden. Per-position accepted tokens per thousand rounds under the forced block: 700, 504, 379, 289, 224, 177, 139 at the drafter's seven positions, unchanged from the control to within a percent, and 9, 7, 5, 5, 4, 4, 3, 3 at the eight lookup-filled positions, which is 1.6 percent of accepted tokens together. So the thirty-fold cliff at the drafter boundary is not the long block rarely existing; the positions exist and accept nothing. The lookup is not starved on ordinary text, it is wrong. The source comment's eight percent at the C1 cohort is falsified at this threshold, and the registered discriminator, a share under two percent at the highest threshold, is met already at the lowest. The remaining thresholds and the card-0 rerun of the two end cells complete it.

**Arm 1, three widths.** Width 4 against width 7: tokens per step 3.140 against 3.757, minus 0.617 at t of minus 7.24; decode rate 133.6 against 159.4, minus 25.7 at t of minus 7.05. Width 5 sits between. The per-position counts at the positions that exist are the same at every width to within two percent, so a narrower block only removes a tail that was paying. Widths 3 and 6 to come; 6 against 7 is the last margin that could differ.

**What the two arms say together, for the question this arc was opened on.** On ordinary text the drafter's seven positions all earn their verify cost and the eight positions above them earn nothing, so of the fifteen verify positions the launcher configures, more than half buy nothing, and the adaptive policy is right to keep them out most of the time. The headroom is not in the width. It is in acceptance at the drafter's own positions, which is where the truncation, noise-stream and block-verification arms are looking.

### Arm 3 closed by its own bound, and the next question on the other box (September 6, late)

After four instrument iterations and three review rounds, arm 3 is closed with the result its reviewers narrowed it to. The drafter truncation switch is inert in shipped production, since the launcher omitted the field that enables probabilistic drafting. Where it is live, its effect on expected acceptance is bounded above by the selector's probability mass outside the kept set at the actual temperature, and that mass is unmeasured; at top-p 0.95 over a sixteen-candidate selector it is plausibly small. So the switch is a knob nobody should bother turning, and the arm's durable output is the counter discipline it forced: alignment proved from source, magnitude from controls with a nonconstant known answer, firing rate from a round-count invariant, a device-side record with a host drain as the instrument shape, and a control miss treated as a stop. Its final rank-cut control failed with per-position values constant across 535 passes, which is the fingerprint of an indexing read rather than of data, and stays a stop.

What the other box runs next is the question this arc was opened on and neither box has measured: the MTP against DFlash2 decomposition. Each method at its own default configuration on the cohort, accepted tokens per verify step, milliseconds per verify step from wall clock over steps, and their quotient, at least two boots per method with order counterbalanced, per-position acceptance and resolved configuration beside every boot. If DFlash2 wins on accepted tokens per step at similar step cost, its edge is the drafter and travels; if it wins on step cost, the edge is that a separate small drafter is cheaper per step than heads inside the target, which is the part that depends on the card. One asymmetry is part of the answer already: on 0.28.0 as shipped, MTP does not sample from a draft distribution and is outside the noise-coupling defect, while probabilistic DFlash2 is inside it until the upstream salt is applied.

### MTP against DFlash2, decomposed: the edge is width, and one cell decides whether width is the lever (September 6, late)

First boot pair on the native 3090, each method at its own default, the cohort at 1024 output tokens, field set on the DFlash2 cell:

| method | accepted per verify step | milliseconds per step | tokens per second | round size |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MTP, 4 wide | 2.218 | 24.60 | 130.8 | 4.00 |
| DFlash2, 7 wide | 3.116 | 25.70 | 160.2 | 7.32 |

DFlash2 accepts 40 percent more per step and costs 4.5 percent more per step, for 22 percent more throughput. The edge is yield, not a cheaper step, so it is not the bandwidth-bound story that would make it a property of these cards; a cheaper drafter would show the opposite sign on step cost. The step-cost figure is if anything overstated, because wall time amortises prefill over fewer rounds for the method that needs fewer.

The per-position curves say where the yield comes from. At the positions both methods have, they are within a point of each other: 81.0 against 80.1 percent at position zero, 62.5 against 63.2 at position one, 45.7 against 49.5, 32.7 against 38.7. On those four positions MTP accepts 2.218 per step and DFlash2 2.316. DFlash2's remaining 0.764 accepted per step comes from positions four to six, which it has and MTP does not, still accepting at 31, 25 and 20 percent. So of an 0.86 lead, 0.10 is being better on shared positions and 0.76 is having three more.

That joins arm 1 and arm 2 into one account: a drafter's per-position curve is a property of the drafter and invariant to block width; every drafter position through the sixth earns its verify cost; the eight lookup positions past the drafter earn nothing; and the gap between the two methods is width, not per-position quality.

**One confound remains and one boot resolves it.** MTP runs four wide by its own default, so width and method are confounded by construction. The launcher's MTP configuration takes the same width setting, so MTP at seven is one environment change. Registered before it runs: if MTP at seven accepts at positions four to six near DFlash2's rates, the lever is purely width and the recommendation is to run either method wider; if MTP collapses past position three while its step cost rises, DFlash2's edge is a block-trained drafter that stays useful at depth, and width was the visible symptom. The lean is toward the second, because arm 1's width invariance is what block training buys, but the lean is not the result. One asymmetry belongs beside the table as a column: on 0.28.0 as shipped MTP is lossless and probabilistic DFlash2 is not until the upstream noise-stream salt is applied.
