## Arm 1 closed: draft width, five boots, monotone on both metrics (2026-09-05)

Card 0, DFLASH_TOKENS = 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 (the drafter's trained block is 8, so 7 is the full width and there is no lookup segment in any cell), field set, prefix caching on, production sampling, cohort 1024 x 8, four seeds. Reference is w7. Within-boot row pairs give the t values; they are not boot replicates and overstate certainty, so the evidence is the direction of every step, not the t.

| width | tok/step | tok/s | ms/step | d tok/step vs w7 | d tok/s vs w7 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | 3.757 | 159.4 | 23.6 | | |
| 6 | 3.556 | 148.0 | 24.0 | -0.202 | -11.4 |
| 5 | 3.375 | 141.8 | 23.8 | -0.382 | -17.5 |
| 4 | 3.140 | 133.6 | 23.5 | -0.617 | -25.7 |
| 3 | 2.814 | 115.4 | 24.4 | -0.943 | -44.0 |

Per-position acceptance per 1000 rounds barely moves with width: position 0 sits at 703 to 728, position 1 at 495 to 548, position 2 at 375 to 417 across all five boots. A position is accepted at its own rate whether or not positions follow it, and the step cost (ms/step, the third column) is flat within 4 percent from width 3 to width 7. So every drafted position pays for itself and the verify cost of the extra positions is invisible at this batch size on this card. The registered idea behind the arm, that trimming the rarely-accepted late positions might buy wall clock, is falsified: the late positions cost nothing to verify and still accept 6 to 9 percent of the time.

What this says about the question Michael asked (3.3 of 7 seems low): the width is not the limiter and neither is the verify cost. The limiter is the drafter's trained block. A drafter trained for a wider block would be the lever, and that is a training artefact nobody on the repo has, not a config change.

## MTP at 7 is void: the checkpoint pins MTP at 4 (other box, 2026-09-05)

The deciding cell for H-width versus H-drafter was registered with a void condition (if the MTP path clamps or refuses 7, the RESOLVED line says so and the cell is void rather than misread). It fired. With DFLASH_TOKENS=15 and with DFLASH_TOKENS=7 the engine resolves num_speculative_tokens to 4; the checkpoint config carries text_config.mtp_num_hidden_layers = 1. The launcher passes the knob at line 432 and the engine overrides it. So MTP cannot be run wider on this checkpoint, H-width versus H-drafter stays unresolved, and the practical finding is the one a reader needs: the DFlash2 lead over MTP (+40.5 percent accepted per step, +22.5 percent tok/s, at +4.5 percent ms/step, with 0.764 of the 0.86 accepted-per-step lead coming from positions 4 to 6 existing at all) is not closable by tuning MTP. Both methods replay bit-identically across boots on that box (m-mtp1b equals m-mtp2; m-dfl1 equals m-dfl2, every counter, only the clock moving), so those numbers are exact for the configuration and carry no sampling error; all their uncertainty is generalisation to other prompts, seeds, and hardware. The +4.5 percent ms/step is being re-measured there with a prefill probe (a max_tokens=1 request on the same prompt and seed ahead of each measured request, so decode-only step cost is the difference).

## Boots on this box mostly replay, and do not always (2026-09-05)

Prompted by the other box's bit-identical boot pairs, every result file here was checked against every other for rows (seed, prompt) with identical drafts, accepted, drafted-tokens, and output-token counts. No two boots on this box are bit-identical. Three pairs replay most rows and diverge on a minority:

| pair | what differs between the boots | identical rows |
|---|---|---|
| cc0 vs coh-flag | nothing in the config (two boots of one configuration) | 28 of 32 |
| cc4096 vs cc8192 | a threshold that rarely fires | 19 of 25 |
| pol-sticky0 vs pol-zrev | policy knobs that leave most rows untouched | 20 of 32 |

So on this box the boot has a little variance where the other box has none. Hypothesis, not a finding: the autotune winner set changes per boot here (#75 reply: 8 boots, 8 distinct winner sets, 5 distinct trajectories), and a different winning kernel flips a rounding tie in a minority of trajectories; the other box's bit-identity would then mean its two boots resolved the same winner set (a compile-cache hit), which its two server logs can confirm or refute directly. If the winner sets there differ and the trajectories still match, the hypothesis is wrong.

Consequence for the block-verification cells, which are running now: the four boots std1, blk1, blk2, std2 are read first as a row-identity matrix. std1 versus std2 gives the box's replay rate, the control. blk versus std at or near that rate means block verification is a no-op on this path (rejection_sampler.py:96-97 wires the method into the V2 sampler, but a no-op would be the finding and it would be a real one). blk versus std well below the control rate means block verification changes acceptance decisions, and only then do the pooled aggregates (tok/step, tok/s, round) mean anything. The earlier "two boot pairs, descriptive at n=2" framing is withdrawn: a boot pair on this box is mostly a replay, and the sizing it was meant to give does not exist. Script: flightbench results/raw/v028/analyze_block.py.

Addendum, same day: the other box's bit-identity turned out to come from a warm persisted AOT compile cache (both its boots log "Directly load AOT compilation" for the same two artefacts). Checked here: the card-0 volume holds 21 AOT entries and the card-1 volume 27, so boots on this box load AOT the same way and still diverge on a minority of rows. AOT warmth is therefore not what separates the boxes; the runtime Triton autotune race (#75) is the remaining candidate here, with its cache separate from inductor's. The other box's boots are about 8 rows each; at this box's per-row divergence of 4 in 32, two 8-row boots match on every row with probability about 0.34, so bit-identity at that n is compatible with the same flip rate and is not evidence of zero variance. The discriminating test, registered before it runs: two warm boots on that box with TRITON_PRINT_AUTOTUNING=1, winner sets compared before rows. Different winners with matching rows means the identity is small-n; identical winners means Triton did not re-race there and a cold-cache boot is the next test.

Operational note: the block cells and the card-0 cheapctx cells collided at boot (both scripts waited on the same condition and cleared in the same 30 second window when w6 exited); both were killed at zero rows and relaunched as one sequential chain, block cells first. Two waiters on one condition is a race; chain them.

## Arm 2 closed: the cliff is real and the dead branch buys nothing (2026-09-05)

Card 1, four boots, DFLASH_TOKENS=15, field set, VLLM_DFLASH2_LOOKUP_CHEAP_CTX at 0, 4096, 8192 and 16384, reference cc0, 32 paired rows per cell. The threshold forces the long verify block below the given context length, so every non-zero cell verifies 15 positions on every step (round 15.000 against 7.17 with the adaptive length).

| cell | tok/step | d tok/step vs cc0 | t | positions 7 to 14, share of accepted |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| cc0 | 3.739 | | | 0.8 percent |
| cc4096 | 3.769 | +0.029 | 0.58 | 1.7 percent |
| cc8192 | 3.766 | +0.026 | 0.51 | 1.6 percent |
| cc16384 | 3.758 | +0.019 | 0.36 | 1.6 percent |

H2 held its registered prediction (the share of positions 7 to 14 stays under about 2 percent at every threshold). H1, the source comment's +8 percent for forcing the long block at short context, is falsified for tokens per step at every threshold: the gain is under 0.03 tokens per step, within noise. Positions 7 to 14 exist and accept, at about 1.6 percent of everything accepted. The wall-clock half of H1 cannot be read on card 1 (four lanes; every width costs about 90 ms per step there because the card is waiting, not computing), so the card-0 cells c0cc0 and c0cc16384 are queued for it.

## The 15-token configuration costs 2.4x per step on card 0, and the cause is not yet attributed (2026-09-05)

Found through the block-verification cells, which run DFLASH_TOKENS=15 (the launcher default is 7; the README calls 15 "reproduction mode" and measures it at +1 percent on chat on the maintainer's hardware). Per-prompt step cost (wall over rounds) for seed 1 on card 0, steady state after the first two prompts of each boot, which carry warm-up in every configuration:

| configuration | prompts 2 to 7, ms per step | boot |
|---|---|---|
| DFLASH_TOKENS=7 (w7) | 23.1 to 23.6 | one |
| DFLASH_TOKENS=15, standard rejection (std1) | 56.2 to 56.8 | one |
| DFLASH_TOKENS=15, block rejection (blk1) | 56.3 to 57.5 | another |

Two boots agree to 1 percent per prompt. Card 0 sampled under blk1: PCIe x8 Gen4, 2745 MHz, 168 W, 98 percent utilisation, so this is not the four-lane state and not host contention from the other card (which was running during std1 and idle for most of blk1). The tokens per step are unchanged (3.739 in std1 against 3.739 in the same configuration on card 1), so the cohort pays 2.4x per step for positions that give it 1.6 percent of its accepted tokens. On card 1 the 7-token and 15-token configurations cost the same (about 90 to 96 ms per step) because that card's step is bounded by its link, which is why this did not show in a week of card-1 runs. The earlier sentence in this document, "under an identical configuration card 0 decodes at about 162 tokens per second", was measured with the 7-token configuration and stands; nothing has degraded.

Not yet known: whether the cost is the lookup's host-side work, the 15-wide verify or drafter path, or a boundary at the first position past the trained block. Registered before the run, in .lookup_cost.sh, three card-0 cells queued behind the block cells: lk15off (DFLASH_TOKENS=15 with VLLM_DFLASH2_LOOKUP=0), t8 and t11. lk15off near 23 ms puts the cost in the lookup; lk15off near 56 ms puts it in the wide path; t8 paying most of it makes it a step function at the boundary. Whichever it is, it is a note for the maintainer with a number attached, and it says nothing yet about his hardware, where the README's own measurement found 15 nearly free.

## Block verification, first read: it changes decisions on every row (2026-09-05, in progress)

std1 against blk1, the first pair, on (drafts, accepted, drafted tokens, output tokens): 0 of 24 rows identical, against a same-configuration replay rate on this box of 28 of 32. So the flag is wired and live on this path, and the pooled comparison will mean something. Direction is mixed row by row (prompt 0: 769 accepted in 258 rounds standard against 755 in 268 block; prompt 2: 476 in 548 against 511 in 519). The control pair std1 against std2 and the second treatment boot blk2 are running; the matrix and the aggregates follow. The step cost per prompt is identical between the two methods to 1 percent, so whatever block verification does to acceptance it does at no cost per step.

Process note, recorded because it is the failure this arc keeps naming: a message to the other box claimed the first ten rows of std1 and blk1 were identical before the comparison had been run; the comparison, run minutes later, gave 0 of 24. The correction went out on the same channel with the rows. The step-cost agreement to 1 percent was real and was mistaken for trajectory agreement.

Seat note: the second fresh-eyes review of this arc (Sol's seat) hit the codex usage limit before producing anything and is deferred until the limit resets; a fresh Claude reviewer with no context from this session is running the same brief in the meantime.

## Configuration label for every 15-token number above (2026-09-05)

The launcher default is DFLASH_TOKENS=7 (start_qwen.sh: DRAFT_TOKENS=${DFLASH_TOKENS:-7}). At 7 the adaptive verify length never engages, because it requires the drafter block to be smaller than the speculative step count, so there are no lookup positions and none of the lookup tail exists. DFLASH_TOKENS=15 is the opt-in reproduction mode the README describes for context-quoting workloads. The cells in this arc that ran at 15, inherited from the issue 73 bisect where the maintainer's report used it: arm 2 (all four cheap-context cells), the block-verification cells std1, blk1, blk2 and std2, the card-0 cheap-context cells, and the other box's MTP-against-DFlash2 decomposition. Arm 1 ran at 3 to 7 and is unaffected. So the lookup-tail findings (positions 7 to 14 at 1.6 percent of accepted tokens; 2.4x step cost on card 0) describe reproduction mode, an opt-in setting with a measured price on this cohort, not the configuration users run by default, and that price is a large part of why the fork defaults to 7. The block-verification pair is being repeated at 7 (std7, blk7, queued behind the attribution cells), and the other box is repeating the decomposition at 7, so that every number that reaches the maintainer or the human describes the shipped default unless labelled otherwise.

Block verification, first pair complete (std1 against blk1, 32 rows each, reproduction mode): tokens per step 3.739 against 3.745, a difference of +0.006 with 16 of 32 rows positive and a row standard deviation of 0.287; decode rate 64.9 against 65.0; round 7.093 against 7.114; per-position rates within a point at every position. Zero rows identical, so the method is live and changes decisions, and the expected acceptance does not move. The claimed 5 to 8 percent (0.19 to 0.30 tokens per step at this base) is excluded on this cohort at 15: the row standard error is about 0.05. The second pair and the pair at the default width follow.

## MTP against DFlash2 at the launcher default, other box (2026-09-05)

The decomposition was re-run at DFLASH_TOKENS=7 after the reproduction-mode relabelling; the registered check passed (the 7-token cell reports positions 0 to 6 only, so the configuration took). Same cohort, same card, one boot per configuration; boots replay on that card, so these are exact for the configuration and carry no sampling error, and all their uncertainty is generalisation.

| configuration | accepted per step | ms per step | tok/s | round |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MTP, 4 wide, the checkpoint's fixed width | 2.218 | 24.6 | 130.8 | 4.00 |
| DFlash2 at 7, launcher default | 2.917 | 25.1 | 156.4 | 7.00 |
| DFlash2 at 15, reproduction mode | 3.116 | 25.7 | 160.2 | 7.32 |

At the default, DFlash2 accepts 31.5 percent more per step than MTP at 1.8 percent more cost per step, for 19.6 percent more decode throughput. The edge is yield, and the yield is positions 4 to 6 existing at all, which MTP cannot have on this checkpoint. Caveat carried from the other box: the ms per step figures for MTP and DFlash2 at 15 still amortise prefill over rounds (the prefill probe was added after those boots); the bias inflates the higher-acceptance arm, so the direction is safe and the cost term is being re-measured with one more MTP boot under the probe.

On that card reproduction mode costs 2.5 percent more per step than the default and returns 2.4 percent more throughput. On this box's card 0 it costs 140 percent more per step. The two cards run the default at nearly the same step time (25.1 there, 23.3 here), so this is not the lane-bound card hiding the extra work; it is a cost this box pays and the native Linux box does not. The candidate is WSL2 host-to-device traffic on the lookup path, which writes draft tokens to the device and reads matches every step, each such synchronisation costing milliseconds under WSL2 rather than microseconds. The attribution cells discriminate: lk15off separates lookup from width; t8 separates a per-synchronisation step function (one lookup position paying the whole 33 ms) from a per-position cost (one position paying an eighth). If it is the WSL2 path, the note for the maintainer is that reproduction mode costs 2.4x per step under WSL2 and about 2.5 percent native on this cohort, which is more useful than either number alone.

Two additions from the research drains (2026-09-05). First, the "artefact nobody has" in the arm 1 conclusion needs a narrower sentence: a block-16 DFlash2 drafter is published for a different target (`z-lab/Muse-Glimmer-30B-DFlash2`, config identical to the block-8 Qwen3.8-27B drafter except `block_size` and `target_layer_ids`, per the research brief), so the drafter class exists at block 16 and the artefact missing is one trained for this target; the same model card says its drafter was finetuned from an existing DFlash v1 drafter, so warm-starting from the block-8 checkpoint is precedented practice rather than a guess. Second, vLLM 0.28.0 ships `VLLM_TRITON_FORCE_FIRST_CONFIG`, which skips the Triton autotune race for the first valid config. That is a direct test of the boot-replay hypothesis, and card 1 was idle, so it is running now: two boots in the cc0 configuration plus the flag (ff1, ff2, script `.ffc_arms.sh`). Registered before the run: ff1 against ff2 identical on 32 of 32 rows means the race is the divergence source on this box; about 28 of 32 means it is not. Secondary readings: tokens per step unchanged against cc0 if the hypothesis is right (the winners differ in speed, not in what is accepted), decode rate unregistered (first-config kernels on the lane-bound card).

Block verification, second treatment boot in (2026-09-05): blk2 reads 3.749 tokens per step, 65.0 tok/s, round 7.117, per-position within a point of blk1. blk1 against blk2 is 27 of 32 rows identical, which is the same-configuration replay rate on card 0 measured inside this experiment (the 28 of 32 above came from card 1 pairs), so the control rate is now known on the card the treatment runs on. std2 follows; the matrix and the second pair close with it.

Instrument note, recorded before anyone reads a card-1 link value from a RESOLVED line: nvidia-smi inside a container started with a single device still lists both cards, so a RESOLVED line that takes the first nvidia-smi row reports card 0 whichever card the cell runs on. The card-1 force-first-config cell printed "pcie_x8" for that reason; the host reading at the same moment was card 1 at 4 lanes, Gen4, 99 percent utilisation, as always. The 30 second link sampler in the queued card-0 cells prints both rows. Also for the record, since the write-up will name hardware: both cards in this box are RTX 4090 (card 0 negotiating 8 lanes, card 1 negotiating 4); the other box is a 3090 on native Linux. The launcher default runs at 23.3 ms per step on the 4090 here and 25.1 on the 3090 there.

Early observation from the first force-first-config boot on card 1, not a finding until ff2 and the card-0 pair land: on the first four prompts ff1 ran 15 to 40 percent faster per step than the autotuned cc0 on the same card (prompt 3, steady state: 77 ms per step against 91), with no first-prompt warm-up penalty because there is no race to run. Trajectories differ from cc0 on every row so far, as expected from different kernels. If the card-0 pair at the default (ff7a, ff7b, queued last) shows tokens per step unchanged and decode rate equal or better against w7, the flag removes the per-boot variance issue 75 describes at no cost and becomes a candidate default for the fork; that is the registration, with the decode-rate direction left open.

## Corrections from the fresh-eyes review (2026-09-05, docs/reviews/arc2-fresh-eyes-opus.md)

An independent reviewer with no context from this arc reproduced every result table above from the raw rows (all exact) and broke six things in the reasoning and instrumentation. Each is corrected here rather than edited away; the sentences above stand as written so the record shows what was claimed and when.

**Arm 1.** The ms per step column is wall over rounds, and wall carries the 1024-token prefill and client overhead, so it is a step cost plus a prefill term that is smaller at narrow widths (more rounds per row). Subtracting a constant prefill widens the spread: the honest sentence is that step cost does not fall as the verify block narrows, and after removing prefill the narrow arms are the more expensive ones per step. "Flat within 4 percent" is retired. "Tok/step and tok/s both monotone" was one measurement stated twice, since tok/s is tok/step over ms/step by identity; the arm has two facts, tok/step monotone and step cost not falling. "The late positions still accept 6 to 9 percent of the time" quoted the share of accepted tokens, not a rate: the marginal rates at positions 5 and 6 are 18.4 and 14.6 percent of rounds, and conditional on the round reaching them about 80 percent. And one confound the rows cannot close: the launcher's own comment (start_qwen.sh:207-209 in the image's 680-line launcher; 220-222 in this checkout) says the DFlash2 checkpoint always proposes the 7 tokens it was trained for and DFLASH_TOKENS is the verify block, so widths 3 to 6 may have removed verify positions only, with the drafter's cost paid at every width. The recommendation then reads "every verified position pays for itself and the drafter's cost is paid whether or not you verify its proposals", which is a different sentence from the one above and is the one to carry.

**Arm 2.** The registration named a per-position accept rate under 2 percent; the result reported a share of accepted tokens. Read as registered, conditional on the round reaching each position, the drafter's own curve is flat at 69 to 81 percent and rises with depth; position 7, the first lookup position, accepts 6.4 percent under forcing (1.8 percent unforced); positions 8 to 14 accept at 74 to 93 percent once 7 is accepted. So the finding is one cliff at the drafter-to-lookup seam followed by full recovery, not a dead tail: the lookup almost never gets its first position right and is nearly as good as the drafter once started. A boundary or off-by-one at that seam is a live candidate the arm did not consider and is cheap to probe. The aggregate conclusion (forcing buys +0.029 tokens per step, nothing) stands, and the +8 percent is falsified more strongly than stated: with tok/s equal to tok/step over ms/step, an 8 percent gain at flat acceptance would need the step to get 7 percent cheaper while verifying eight more positions. Also, on a cohort whose context never exceeds about 2k, thresholds of 4096, 8192 and 16384 all force unconditionally, so arm 2 is one control boot against one forced condition sampled three times (those three boots replay 31, 24 and 23 of 32 rows against each other, and are the best same-behaviour controls on the box), not four thresholds.

**Block verification.** The row-identity rule's "live" branch proves little for a method claimed lossless: any resampling that consumes the random stream differently decorrelates every row while changing the output distribution not at all, so 0 of 32 is the expected outcome of any implementation that touches the sampler, worthless ones included. What the rule can still say is the other direction (identity at the control rate would have meant a no-op). The engine's acceptance of the key was never shown by the cells; it is now: the built tree defines RejectionSampleMethod as the literal set standard, synthetic, block (config/speculative.py:80) and the V2 worker's rejection sampler sets use_block_verification on "block" (v1/worker/gpu/spec_decode/rejection_sampler.py:96-97, kernels in rejection_sampler_utils.py:947-1170), the live engine logs "Using V2 Model Runner", and the API server's "non-default args" line (api_utils.py:272) records the speculative config dict the engine received. Excerpts: docs/research/installed-tree-excerpts-0.28.0.md. Final matrix at 15, four boots: pair 1 (std1, blk1) +0.006 tokens per step with 16 of 32 rows positive; pair 2 (std2, blk2) +0.022 with 18 of 32; controls std1 against std2 20 of 32 identical, blk1 against blk2 27 of 32; every treatment-against-control pair 0 of 32; step cost identical to 1 percent. Stated properly: one configuration, two boot pairs, +0.014 tokens per step, both pairs positive, each within one row standard error (0.05) of zero, boot variance an order of magnitude below the row term (the three forced arm-2 boots have a boot-mean sd of 0.0055). The method changes every decision and is worth about nothing, in that order: the 0 of 32 rules out an inert flag, so the exclusion of the 5 to 8 percent claim (3.6 to 5.8 standard errors) is a negative with the mechanism demonstrably live, not a null from a cell that might not have engaged. A 1 to 2 percent gain, which would be free throughput since the step cost is method-independent, needs 64 to 258 paired rows and is the question worth asking next. The pair at the launcher default (std7, blk7) is queued.

**The 15-token step cost.** The launcher sets ASYNC_SCHED=0 whenever the lookup is on and DRAFT_TOKENS exceeds 7 (start_qwen.sh:291-296; the adaptive verify length only works on the synchronous scheduling path) and line 658 turns that into --no-async-scheduling. Verified on the real path on both boxes: the running 15-token cell's "non-default args" line here reads async_scheduling False, and the other box's four logs read False at 15 and True at 7 and for MTP. So every 56 ms cell ran synchronous scheduling and every 23 ms cell ran async, and a per-step host round trip that costs microseconds native and milliseconds under WSL2 fits both boxes with one mechanism. The three attribution cells registered above (lk15off, t8, t11) were confounded: lk15off would have restored async scheduling in the same move that removed the lookup, and t8 and t11 sit above 7 with the lookup on. They were withdrawn before running and replaced by a7off (7 with ASYNC_SCHED=0), a15on (15 with ASYNC_SCHED=1, which pads every verify to 15) and lk15off, in that order, with the engine's config lines saved into each output. The other box's "+2.5 percent for reproduction mode" carried the same confound and is withdrawn by its author; a native pair with async pinned equal on both arms is queued there. The launcher also changes MAX_SEQS (8 to 4), MAX_LEN (65536 to 57344), the CUDA graph memory budget and the verify attention's query maximum at the same boundary (lines 236 and 402-410), none of which the registration named.

**Boot replay.** "The replay unit is the card" is withdrawn: the two cards had not run the same configuration (w7 is 7 tokens with async on; cc0 is 15 with async off, on another volume), so the cross-card 0 of 32 carried no weight. c0cc0, running now, is the first genuine cross-card same-configuration pair. Same-behaviour replay on this box is a distribution, 20 to 31 of 32, not the single 28. The reviewer names a tree-visible alternative to the autotune hypothesis: prefix caching resumes the GDN recurrent state from the last cached block boundary (start_qwen.sh:495-496), a numerically different path from recomputing, and which rows resume depends on the boot's history. Registered and queued on card 1 behind the force-first-config pair: two boots with prefix caching off (pc0a, pc0b); 32 of 32 identical means the cache is the source, about 28 means it is not. Atomic-add nondeterminism in the quantised GEMM is excluded by the patches (marlin-tune-table.patch:60 registers use_atomic_add False; the only tl.atomic_add in the fork writes a hit counter).

**MTP at 7.** The void is void. DFLASH_TOKENS is read only inside the SPEC=dflash2 branch (start_qwen.sh:231); the MTP config at line 486 takes DRAFT_TOKENS, which CTX=fast defaults to 4 at line 155. The other box's cells passed DFLASH_TOKENS and observed the launcher's default, not a checkpoint clamp; mtp_num_hidden_layers=1 is one MTP layer chained k times, not a width cap. Struck: "MTP cannot be run wider on this checkpoint", "the DFlash2 lead over MTP is not closable by tuning", and "MTP, 4 wide, the checkpoint's fixed width" in the table above (4 is the launcher's default). The deciding cell is SPEC=mtp DRAFT_TOKENS=7, running on the other box now, with the void condition now meaningful: the engine's resolved num_speculative_tokens in the non-default args line must read 7.

**First force-first-config boot (card 1).** ff1 against cc0, 32 rows: tokens per step 3.681 against 3.739 (paired -0.058, t -0.48, noise), round 7.215 against 7.172, 0 of 32 rows identical (different kernels, as expected), and step cost 77.0 against 92.4 ms, 76.8 against 90.7 in steady state, 15 percent faster with the race skipped. Direction was unregistered and this is one boot on the lane-bound card; ff2 and the card-0 pair at the default decide it. If it holds on card 0, the autotune race on this box is choosing slower kernels than the first valid config, which under WSL2 timing noise would not be surprising, and the flag is both the determinism fix and a speed-up. [Withdrawn 2026-09-05: the neighbour, lane and contamination attributions in this paragraph did not survive; see the section beginning "First, the one-engine-per-host rule loses its evidence" and the corrections from the second fresh-eyes review at the end of this record.]

**Provenance.** Thirteen sentences in the arc-two section cited things a reader could not trace. The installed-tree lines now have an excerpt file (above). The link-state claim has a capture (flightbench results/raw/v028/nvidia-smi-links-20260905T061054Z.txt: card 1 at 4 lanes of 16, card 0 at 8 of 16, both RTX 4090, host side under load). The other box's cells and log lines remain named artefacts on that box, reachable by its operator and not from this tree; the two upstream fixes are cited by PR number and verified in the merged diffs on GitHub, with the salt's absence confirmed by grep in the built tree (excerpt file, empty result under "#54282").

## MTP at width 7 exists, and the drafter is the edge (other box, 2026-09-05)

The deciding cell ran with SPEC=mtp DRAFT_TOKENS=7 and the engine's own non-default args line reads num_speculative_tokens 7, async_scheduling True, method mtp. The knob took. Same cohort, same card, async scheduling on in both arms, one boot each (boots replay on that card).

| position | MTP at 7, accepted per round | DFlash2 at 7, accepted per round | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 78.9 percent | 79.0 percent | 1.00 |
| 1 | 56.9 | 60.1 | 0.95 |
| 2 | 41.4 | 46.4 | 0.89 |
| 3 | 28.7 | 36.4 | 0.79 |
| 4 | 20.1 | 28.4 | 0.71 |
| 5 | 14.6 | 23.3 | 0.63 |
| 6 | 10.9 | 18.2 | 0.60 |

| configuration | accepted per step | ms per step | tok/s |
|---|---|---|---|
| MTP at 4 (launcher default) | 2.218 | 24.6 | 130.8 |
| MTP at 7 | 2.516 | 27.3 | 128.7 |
| DFlash2 at 7 (launcher default) | 2.917 | 25.1 | 156.4 |

Three readings, registered as H-width against H-drafter before the cell and decided by it. First, the two drafters are indistinguishable at position 0 and the ratio falls monotonically with depth to 0.60 at position 6: a block-trained drafter stays useful at depth and chained distilled heads do not, so width was the visible symptom of the drafter difference, not its cause. Second, widening MTP from 4 to 7 buys 13.4 percent more accepted per step and costs 11.1 percent more per step, netting slightly worse throughput; running MTP wider is available and not worth doing, which is a measured statement where the struck one was inferred from a configuration that never took. Third, at matched width DFlash2 is cheaper per step than MTP (25.1 against 27.3 ms), so the earlier "DFlash2 pays a little per step for its yield" framing was an artefact of comparing 7 positions against 4: at equal width the separate small drafter beats chaining the in-target layer seven times, and DFlash2 wins both terms.

For the question this arc was opened on (is DFlash2's lead over MTP real, or a property of these cards): it is real and it is the drafter. At the widths users run it is +31.5 percent accepted per step against MTP's default; at matched width it is +15.9 percent accepted per step and 8.3 percent cheaper per step; and the gap opens with depth exactly as a block-trained drafter against distilled heads predicts. The ms per step figures for the two width-7 cells carry the prefill probe (decode-only step cost); the MTP-at-4 figure predates the probe and is amortised over rounds, as noted above.

## The first genuine cross-card pair, and boots that match exactly or not at all (2026-09-05)

c0cc0 ran on card 0 in the cc0 configuration (15 tokens, lookup on, cheap-context 0, field set, prefix caching on, which the launcher runs with synchronous scheduling), the same configuration as std1 and std2 on card 0 and as cc0 on card 1. Row identity on (drafts, accepted, drafted tokens, output tokens):

| pair | cards | identical rows |
|---|---|---|
| c0cc0 against cc0 | 0 against 1 | 0 of 32 |
| c0cc0 against std2 | 0 against 0 | 32 of 32 |
| c0cc0 against std1 | 0 against 0 | 20 of 32 |
| std1 against std2 | 0 against 0 | 20 of 32 |

So the withdrawn sentence is reinstated on evidence: with the configuration held equal, the two cards share no row and one card replays itself, so the replay unit is the card (each card has its own cache volume, so card and volume cannot be separated here; nothing in a volume plausibly changes accepted counts, but it is stated). And the shape of the replay is not a per-row flip rate. c0cc0 and std2 are bit-identical boots, and std1 differs from both on the same twelve rows. A boot either matches another boot exactly or differs from it on the set of rows that are sensitive to whatever differed at boot, which is the shape a per-boot kernel selection produces (one winner set for the boot, twelve trajectories that pass through a rounding tie under it) and not the shape a per-step random source produces. The force-first-config pair on card 1 and the pair at the default on card 0 test that directly; the prefix-cache-off pair tests the alternative.

c0cc0 also replicates the reproduction-mode step cost on card 0 with a third boot: 56.5 ms per step in steady state against std1's 56.5, tokens per step 3.727, decode 64.7 tok/s, round 7.135. Card 1 was running the second force-first-config boot during its rows, so host contention is still not excluded by this cell; it is excluded by the three boots agreeing to under 1 percent across different card-1 states.

## Reproduction mode with scheduling held equal, native (other box, 2026-09-05)

The other box re-ran width 15 against width 7 with ASYNC_SCHED=0 passed explicitly on both arms, so scheduling is held equal by construction; both arms engine-confirmed from the non-default args line (num_speculative_tokens 15 and 7, async_scheduling False on both).

| arm | accepted per step | ms per step | tok/s | round |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| width 15, sync | 3.116 | 25.5 | 161.1 | 7.31 |
| width 7, sync | 2.900 | 25.5 | 153.1 | 7.00 |
| width 7, async (the launcher default, from the decomposition above) | 2.917 | 25.1 | 156.4 | 7.00 |

With scheduling equal, width 15 costs 0.3 percent more per step natively and returns 7.5 percent more accepted per step on that cohort, 5.2 percent more throughput. The earlier unpinned pair, which moved scheduling with width, read +2.5 percent per step and +2.4 percent throughput, so the launcher's forced switch to synchronous scheduling above 7 drafts costs about 2 to 3 points of throughput on native Linux at batch 1 (153.1 against 156.4 at width 7 is 2.1 percent; the pinned-minus-unpinned difference is 2.8). The launcher comment at start_qwen.sh:295 puts that cost under 1 percent; the controlled measurement puts it at two to three times that, same direction. On this box the same switch costs 140 percent per step (56 against 23 ms), and the a7off cell, queued, is the direct measurement of it at width 7.

Two caveats carried from the other box's author. The +7.5 percent accepted per step cannot be attributed to the lookup positions, which supply about 1.5 percent of accepted tokens under a forced block; the two arms have different trajectories and most of the difference is positions 0 to 6 behaving differently between runs. And the gain is the cohort's: on this box's cohort, std1 at 15 (3.739) against w7 at 7 (3.757) shows no gain at all, so what reproduction mode returns is whatever the workload's context repetition gives it, between nothing and the README's +50 percent on verbatim reproduction.

So reproduction mode has three prices and no single one: about +5 percent throughput natively with scheduling held equal, about +2.4 percent as the launcher ships it (it takes the async loss to get the adaptive length), and a 2.4x step cost under WSL2, each on a cohort that repeats some context.

## Same cohort, same sampling, different acceptance: an open box difference (2026-09-05)

The cohort attribution two sections up is withdrawn. Both boxes read the same file (bench/prompts_real.jsonl, sha256 beginning 27da4fd8b2dcc133b98cec54bc337c34, 3281 bytes, 8 prompts of 180 to 1211 characters) and both sample at the model's generation_config (temperature 1.0, top_k 20, top_p 0.95; the server logs that it applied it, and the other box sends the same values explicitly). Yet the levels and the width response differ:

| | this box (card 0, 4090, WSL2) | other box (3090, native) |
|---|---|---|
| width 7, tokens per step | 3.757 (one boot) | 3.900 |
| width 15, tokens per step | 3.739, 3.727, 3.727 (three boots) | 4.116 |
| response to width 15 | -0.5 percent | +5.5 percent |
| round at width 15 | 7.09 to 7.14 | 7.31 |

The long block is entered on both boxes and about a third as often here. Candidates, cheapest first: the fork's int8 activation quantisation, which every cell here runs (INT8_ACT=int8 on mlp, linear_attn and self_attn, PREFILL_ATTN=int8) and which the other box may not; the drafter was distilled against the unquantised target, so an int8-activation target moves the verify distribution, and fewer accepts at every position with a lower lookup entry rate is what that predicts. Then the card (Marlin kernels differ by SM and their numerics with them), then anything else that differs in the two engines' non-default args lines, which are being compared directly. One cell decides the first candidate: width 7 with INT8_ACT unset on card 0 against w7 (queued as w7bf, registered: tokens per step rises toward 3.90 if int8 activations are the cause; stays near 3.76 if not). Until it runs, every acceptance level in this document is labelled with its box, and the two boxes' width responses are not pooled.

Box difference, candidates excluded by configuration rather than measurement (2026-09-05, later): int8 activation quantisation is excluded, the other box runs INT8_ACT=int8 on the same three layer groups with PREFILL_ATTN=int8, identically. The drafter checkpoint is identical (config.json and model.safetensors sha256 match on both boxes; dflash_config block 8, conv group 16, kernel 2, selector rank 256 and top-k 16, target layers 5, 19, 33, 47, 61). The fork trees are identical where it matters: the other box's cb5679d is this image's 6869c80 plus a README line and the merge commit, with no difference under patches/ or single-user/. The KV pool is pinned equal (kv_cache_memory_bytes 5583457484 in both engines' non-default args lines; gpu_memory_utilization 0.90 against 0.93 is headroom above a pinned pool), so the pool-size hypothesis for the different lookup entry rates falls. The two engines' non-default args lines match on every other field compared. What remains is the target checkpoint, being fingerprinted on both sides, and the silicon: a 4090 under WSL2 against a 3090 native, which selects different Marlin and Triton kernels and therefore different numerics in the target's verify and in the drafter's context path. The discriminating statistic needs no new cell: per-position acceptance conditional on reaching the position, width 7 with async scheduling on, both boxes (this box's w7: 70.7, 72.6, 75.4, 77.4, 78.2, 80.7, 79.6 percent at positions 0 to 6). A gap uniform across positions is target numerics moving every verify the same way; a gap that grows with depth is the drafter's own context path differing between cards. The bf16-activation pair registered above is withdrawn unrun, its premise excluded.

The target checkpoint is identical too (all ten files match byte for byte), so the two boxes share cohort, sampling, drafter, target, tree and engine configuration, and differ in silicon and deployment only. The per-position comparison then came back as a third shape, not one of the two registered. Conditional on reaching the position, width 7 with async scheduling on:

| position | other box (3090 native) | this box (4090 WSL2) | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 79.0 percent | 70.7 | +8.3 |
| 1 | 76.1 | 72.6 | +3.5 |
| 2 | 77.1 | 75.4 | +1.7 |
| 3 | 78.5 | 77.4 | +1.1 |
| 4 | 78.0 | 78.2 | -0.2 |
| 5 | 82.2 | 80.7 | +1.5 |
| 6 | 77.9 | 79.6 | -1.7 |

The gap is at position 0 and vanishes by position 4. Two things follow. The other box's conditional rate is flat at about 78 percent across all seven positions, so the steep unconditional decay (79, 60, 46, 36, 28, 23, 18) is multiplicative, a constant hazard per position, which is the cleanest statement of the per-position curve in this document: a block-trained drafter is about as good at its seventh position as at its first, given that it got there. On this box the curve rises from 70.7 toward that level, so position 0 is the outlier here in absolute terms as well. In round terms: 29.7 percent of this box's verify rounds accept nothing against 21 percent there, and with the rest of the curve matching, that is the whole gap between the boxes' acceptance levels.

Position 0 is the only position conditioned on a verified prefix; every later one conditions on unverified drafts. Candidates, ordered by cost to check: the request endpoint (this box's client posts a raw prompt to /v1/completions with no chat template, so the model continues bare text; if the other box's client uses chat completions the drafted text differs in kind at every round start and nowhere else, which fits the shape; asked); the bonus token (position 0 conditions on the previous round's bonus token, sampled from the target's residual at full sampling, so anything that makes that sample more surprising on one box lands only at position 0; a greedy cell on each box separates it, because at temperature 0 the bonus token is the argmax on both: the gap closing greedy means sampling of the bonus, staying means the drafter's first step); and the drafter's first step itself (the selector's first proposal after a verified token runs a different index path from later steps). Kernel numerics in the target are not favoured: a numerics difference would have to be confined to the first verify position, which is not what numerics do. A greedy width-7 cell is queued on card 1 behind the prefix-cache pair (g7, client override temperature 0, counters only).

The endpoint is excluded (both clients post a raw prompt to /v1/completions, no chat template, no thinking block) and so is the text regime (distinct-word ratio 0.537 there against about 0.55 here, gzip ratio 0.425 against 0.45). So the finding is narrower than "acceptance differs between cards" and survives whatever the mechanism turns out to be: the two boxes differ in how often a verify round dies immediately, 29.7 percent of rounds here against 21.0 percent there, and not in how the drafter continues once a round has started. The bonus-token candidate is the only one on the list that is structurally confined to one position. The greedy pair (g-t0 there, g7 here, both width 7 at temperature 0 on the same cohort and endpoint) is registered on both boxes with the same reading, and it gives a second answer for free: at temperature 0 the trajectory is deterministic given the prompt, so if the boxes still diverge row by row at greedy, that is a target-numerics difference between the cards shown directly, independent of the position-0 question.

## Two registered tests answered, one against the hypothesis (2026-09-05, 06:35Z)

**The cliff's wall-clock half, on the fast card.** c0cc16384 against c0cc0, card 0, 32 paired rows, both cells synchronous scheduling by the launcher rule: forcing the long block costs 1.4 ms per step more (57.8 against 56.5 in steady state, paired t 36) and returns 0.075 tokens per step more (t 1.6, within noise), for a decode rate of 64.4 against 64.7 tok/s. The reviewer's registered expectation (a positive step-cost delta on card 0, larger than card 1's) holds: 2.5 percent here against 1.7 percent on the lane-bound card. H1's wall-clock half is falsified on both cards; forcing the long block buys nothing at this context length. The seam cliff replicates on card 0: conditional on reaching the position, the forced cell accepts 68.9 to 79.3 percent at positions 0 to 6, 5.2 percent at position 7, and 70.3 to 96.0 percent at positions 8 to 14. [Withdrawn 2026-09-05: the neighbour, lane and contamination attributions in this paragraph did not survive; see the section beginning "First, the one-engine-per-host rule loses its evidence" and the corrections from the second fresh-eyes review at the end of this record.]

**The force-first-config replay test fails its registration.** ff1 against ff2 on card 1, same configuration, Triton autotune race skipped in both (the flag confirmed installed in both logs): 5 of 32 rows identical, against 28 of 32 for the autotuned same-configuration pair on the same card. The registered prediction was 32 of 32. So skipping the race makes boots less reproducible, not more, and the per-boot divergence on this box is not the Triton autotune race, or not only. Whatever varies between boots survives a deterministic kernel choice. The remaining candidates are the ones registered beside it: prefix caching with the GDN state resumed from a cached block boundary (the pc0a and pc0b pair, booting now on card 1), and the inductor combo-kernel benchmarking that the engine's compilation config shows enabled (benchmark_combo_kernel True), which is a second race the flag does not touch. The ff cells' decode rates are not read: ff2 ran while this box was hashing fifteen gigabytes of checkpoint for the fingerprint above and while card 0 was measuring, and its steady-state step cost (143.7 ms against ff1's 76.8) is that contention. Counters are unaffected; the tokens per step of the two boots (3.681, 3.798) differ by more than any same-configuration pair above, which is the same finding as the identity count.

**The position-0 comparison is on hold.** The other box's cells send a prefill probe (same prompt and seed, max_tokens 1) immediately before each measured request, with prefix caching on, so every measured generation there starts from a warm prefix cache and a GDN state resumed from a cached block boundary, while every generation here starts cold. A warm-start effect would touch the first round of a generation and nothing after it, which is the signature attributed above first to the bonus token and then to the cards. The other box is running the greedy cell with and without the probe; until that pair lands, the 21.0 against 29.7 percent zero-accept comparison is unread, and the width response there (both arms carry the probe equally) is internally consistent but its absolute levels are warm-start numbers. This is the fourth instrument artefact of the arc and the first that would have produced a positive finding about silicon.

Amendment, recorded before the card-0 force-first-config pair runs (06:50Z): after the card-1 result the registered 32 of 32 for ff7a against ff7b is expected to fail; the prediction stands unchanged as a replication on the other card, and the pair keeps its second purpose, the default-width decode rate against w7 on the card whose clock counts, direction unregistered. Script header carries the same amendment.

The hold is lifted (2026-09-05, 06:55Z): the other box ran its greedy width-7 cell with and without the prefill probe and the two are identical on every row and every counter (8 of 8 rows, per-position 1554, 1210, 972, 764, 598, 488, 407 in both, 7931 tokens in both, only the clock moving). The probe warms nothing that matters, the cache-resumption reading of the position-0 gap is wrong on that box, and the position-0 numbers above are read as valid. The other box's greedy baseline, ready for this box's g7: width 7, temperature 0, 1938 rounds, conditional-on-reaching 80.2, 77.9, 80.3, 78.6, 78.3, 81.6, 83.4 percent at positions 0 to 6, zero-accept rounds 19.8 percent against 21.0 sampled. Sampled against greedy barely moves that box at all, including at position 0, which is where the bonus-token mechanism predicted movement if sampling were the channel; so that story is already weakened from one side. The reading for g7, registered as before: zero-accept near this box's sampled 29.7 percent means sampling is not the channel on either box and the gap is in the drafter's first step or the target's logits at a verified prefix; a drop toward 20 percent means the bonus token's sampling is depressed on this box specifically, a numerics finding confined to the residual draw.

On the failed force-first-config replay, two readings to separate before the cache pair lands, both from the other box: either the first-listed configuration is itself less numerically stable than the autotuned winners (chosen for compilability, not for tie margins), so a second per-boot input that the winners tolerate flips more rows under it; or the flag changes something else about the build, in which case ff1 and ff2 are not the same build as std1 and std2 and the 5 of 32 measures the flag rather than the race. The engine-config excerpts in the queued card-0 cells will show whether anything beyond the flag differs; either way a second per-boot input exists, since a deterministic kernel choice did not make boots identical.

## The link-sampled repeat, and a boot state that is binary (2026-09-05, 06:45Z)

std1b repeated the std1 cell on card 0 with the link and clocks sampled every 30 seconds into the output: 18 samples, every one at 8 lanes, Gen4, 2745 MHz, about 166 W, 97 to 98 percent utilisation. Steady-state step cost 56.5 ms, the same as std1, std2 and c0cc0. The 2.4x is not the link and not the clocks; it is the synchronous scheduling the launcher selects at this width, as established above, with the a7off cell running now as the direct measurement.

The counters give something new. std1b is bit-identical to std1 on all 32 rows, and differs from std2 and from c0cc0 on the same twelve rows that std1 does. So among four boots of one configuration on one card there are exactly two trajectories: {std1, std1b} and {std2, c0cc0}, and the twelve rows are where they part. The per-boot state that decides a trajectory here is binary, or close to it, and every boot lands in one of the two. That is a much narrower object than "the Triton race picks one of many winner sets", and it fits the failed force-first-config test better than the race did: a binary boot state that persists under a fixed kernel choice would give two ff trajectories as well, and two boots that happened to land on opposite sides would show many differing rows (ff1 against ff2 gave 27), while a pair on the same side would show none. Candidates for a binary state at boot, none tested: a buffer alignment or address parity that selects between two kernel algorithms; the order in which two CUDA graph shapes are captured; a single tie in the combo-kernel benchmark. The prefix-cache-off pair on card 1 reads first; if it comes back identical, the state lives in the cache path. Otherwise the cheap next cell is more boots of one configuration to count the trajectories: two means a bit, more means a race.

The other box partitions too (2026-09-05, 07:05Z). Three boots of its 15-token synchronous configuration on the same eight prompts and seeds form two trajectories: two boots bit-identical to each other and a third differing from them on two of eight rows. Its width-7 greedy pair and its width-15 pair each form one trajectory, so the pairs it had been quoting as zero variance were same-side landings, as the partition predicts, and every "exact for this configuration on that box" above carries the correction: exact for the boot's side of a binary state, with the other side one boot away. One confound it carries and is testing: the odd boot there is the one with the prefill probe, and the probe was shown harmless at width 7 greedy only; two no-probe boots at width 15 are running there. If they split among themselves, the state is a boot-level bit on both boxes and the probe is irrelevant; if all no-probe boots agree and only the probe boot differs, the probe is the cause at that width and an instrument validated in one regime was assumed valid in another, which would be worth publishing on its own. With this box's four-boot partition ({std1, std1b} against {std2, c0cc0}, twelve rows) and the cross-side reading of the force-first-config pair, the object to hunt is one binary decision at boot; address or buffer alignment selecting between two kernel algorithms is the first candidate on both boxes, because it is stable within a process and flips across them. The trajectory count at the default (t7b, t7c, t7d against w7) is queued last on card 0 and the no-probe pair is its native twin.

Correction from the other box, and a re-check here that survives it (2026-09-05, 07:15Z). The other box's two-trajectory claim is retracted by its author: its signature included drafted tokens, the one field known since the order experiments to depend on the adaptive block-length policy's carried state, and once the signature is restricted to what was generated and accepted, five boots of its 15-token configuration (two with the prefill probe, three without) are one trajectory: drafts, accepted and output tokens identical on every prompt, accepted per step 3.1163 on every boot, with drafted tokens moving by about 0.4 percent on two prompts under the probe. The probe changes how many tokens were asked for and nothing about what was generated or accepted; the async-scheduling number above is built from drafts and accepted and is unaffected.

The same correction applied here does not dissolve the partition. With drafted tokens removed from the signature (rounds, accepted, output tokens only): std1 against std1b 32 of 32, std2 against c0cc0 32 of 32, std1 against std2 22 of 32, std1 against c0cc0 22 of 32, blk1 against blk2 28 of 32, ff1 against ff2 6 of 32. The ten rows that separate the two sides differ in rounds and in accepted tokens, not only in drafted tokens (seed 3 prompt 2: 545 rounds and 479 accepted on one side against 583 and 440 on the other; seed 2 prompt 1: 313 and 714 against 319 and 704), and their per-position counters differ with them. So this box's partition is a trajectory partition, and the cross-box difference is now sharp: five boots one trajectory on the 3090 native, four boots two trajectories on the 4090 under WSL2, on identical model files, identical trees and matching engine configuration. Whatever the binary boot state is, that card has it and the other does not. The candidates stand as listed (alignment or address parity selecting between two kernel algorithms first, because it is stable within a process and flips across them); the prefix-cache-off pair and the trajectory count at the default read next.

## a7off: synchronous scheduling costs nothing at width 7 on this box (2026-09-05, 07:20Z)

| cell | width | scheduling | tok/step | steady ms/step | tok/s |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| w7 | 7 | async (launcher default) | 3.798 | 23.3 | 161.2 |
| a7off | 7 | ASYNC_SCHED=0 passed explicitly | 3.727 | 23.2 | 159.0 |
| std1 | 15 | synchronous (launcher rule) | 3.739 | 56.5 | 64.9 |

A repeat with the full line saved is queued last on card 0 (a7off2); the cell is void if that line does not read False. The a15on cell running now is confirmed live from its engine line: async_scheduling True with num_speculative_tokens 15, the arm that has never existed on either box. With sync scheduling free at width 7, the 2.4x lives in the 15-token path itself, and a15on against std1 (async on against off, both 15) and lk15off against a15on (lookup off against on, both 15 and both async on) now separate the verify width from the lookup. On the launcher's "under 1 percent" for losing async scheduling at batch 1: in step cost this box agrees at width 7 (23.2 against 23.3 ms); the native box measured 2.1 percent of throughput at the same width (153.1 against 156.4 tok/s, both engine-confirmed), so the comment is right here and about twice low there. Losing async scheduling at width 7 costs 2.07 percent of throughput and 1.66 percent of step time on the native 3090, and about nothing on this box (23.2 against 23.3 ms). The earlier "2 to 3 points" was a subtraction of two comparisons and carried both their errors; 2.07 percent supersedes it. So the launcher comment ("under 1 percent") is right on one box and low by about two on the other, which does not clear the bar for a maintainer note on its own; if it goes anywhere it is one line inside the width discussion: losing async is worth about two points natively at batch 1 and nothing under WSL2. It also removes the last reason to think the 2.4x at width 15 here is scheduling-related; on the native box the 15-token path costs 0.3 percent more per step than width 7 with scheduling pinned, so there is no 2.4x there to explain, and a15on against std1 here is the arm that says whether this box's cost is the async path or the lookup. ## The cross-box acceptance gap is inside one box's own sampling spread (2026-09-05, 07:45Z)

A correction to the sections above that compared acceptance levels across the boxes. The spread between trajectories on one box is large: per-row tokens per step in w7 has a standard deviation of 0.98 across 32 rows (rows run from 1.67 to 5.45; seed 2 averages 4.02 on the eight prompts where seed 1 averages 3.63, with the same text regime by distinct-word and gzip ratio), so a 32-row mean carries a standard error of about 0.17. The cross-box gap, 3.900 against 3.757, is 0.143, under one standard error of a single box's mean, and the two boxes sampled different trajectories (seeds 1 to 4 here, 1000 plus the prompt index there). The gap is what two draws of 32 trajectories look like; it is not established. The zero-accept comparison is worse: this box's 29.7 percent was pooled over rounds, which weights the low-acceptance rows (they have more rounds) heavily; the row mean is 24.0 percent with a standard error of 2.6, and by seed it runs from 19.8 to 32.7. Whether the other box's 21.0 percent is pooled or a row mean decides which number it compares to, and the position-0 gap may be a statistic mismatch plus sampling. So "the boxes differ in how often a round dies immediately" returns to a hypothesis, and the greedy pair is the only clean cross-box comparison: at temperature 0 each box generates one trajectory per prompt, so identical counters mean identical numerics, and the first token at which two greedy trajectories diverge, if they do, is the numerics difference shown directly. The within-box paired comparisons in this document are unaffected; they pair rows by seed and prompt and their errors are the within-pair ones stated with them.

**The boot-level state, evidenced on both sides.** The other box now has eight boots of one configuration forming one trajectory (drafted tokens excluded from the signature; p 0.008 if a fair bit existed), against this box's four boots forming two, separated on the same ten rows in rounds, accepted tokens and per-position counters. The claim that survives, with its scope: under this configuration, boot-to-boot trajectory variance is present on the 4090 under WSL2 and absent on the 3090 native at n of 8. Not claimed: which of card, driver, WSL2 or container is responsible, since all four are confounded between the boxes and nothing run so far separates them; and not claimed that the native box lacks the state in general, since a bit that only flips under another configuration (a tie in the combo-kernel benchmark, a capture-order choice between graph shapes) would be invisible in eight boots of one. This box's two cards both show boot variance (card 1's same-configuration pairs replay 29, 31 and 24 of 32), so within this host the state is not confined to one card.

**The prefix-cache pair, first boot.** pc0a (cache off, card 1) shares one trajectory in 32 with cc0 (cache on): prefix caching resumes the GDN state from a cached block boundary and that is a different numerical path from recomputing, so a different trajectory on nearly every row is expected and is not the test. Its tokens per step, 3.839 against 3.739, is within the standard error above. The test is pc0b against pc0a; it decides whether cache-off boots replay.

## The prefix-cache pair fails its registration too (2026-09-05, 07:15Z)

pc0a against pc0b, card 1, the cc0 configuration with prefix caching off (engine line enable_prefix_caching False in both): 17 of 32 rows identical trajectories, against 29 of 32 for the cache-on pair on the same card. The registered prediction was 32 of 32. Prefix caching is not the boot-divergence source; turning it off makes boots diverge more. The fifteen differing rows are spread over all four seeds and differ in rounds and accepted tokens (seed 1 prompt 2: 265 rounds and 760 accepted against 300 and 723). Tokens per step are 3.839 and 3.851, so the aggregate is untouched, as everywhere in this thread.

Two interventions have now each removed a candidate and each increased divergence: skipping the Triton autotune race (5 of 32) and turning prefix caching off (17 of 32), against 28 to 31 of 32 for the untouched configuration. That is a pattern, not two nulls. Whatever the per-boot state is, its effect on a trajectory scales with how much computation the boot performs from scratch: with prefix caching on, a prompt's prefill is computed once per boot and its state reused by the other three seeds, so a boot has eight independent prefills; with it off, thirty-two. The forced first config plausibly does the same through more or slower kernels. So the state is upstream of prefill numerics and is applied per computation, and the count of affected rows measures how many independent computations a boot performed, not how strong the state is. Candidates consistent with that: a kernel-algorithm selection made once per boot from two near-equivalent choices (address or buffer alignment at allocation deciding which), applied on every prefill and decode. The trajectory count at the default (t7b, t7c, t7d) shows whether the default configuration partitions into two, and the greedy pair (g7, g7b, temperature 0, text saved) shows whether the state reaches the text.

## lk15off: the 2.4x is the 15-wide path itself (2026-09-05, 07:25Z, first ten rows)

With a7off showing the synchronous scheduler free at width 7 and this cell showing the lookup absent from the cost, what costs 2.4x on this card is the 15-wide verify path as such: something that engages when num_speculative_tokens exceeds the trained block, and that the native 3090 runs for 0.3 percent. Candidates, each with a queued cell on card 0 (script .width15_cause.sh, last in the queue): a boundary at the first position past the trained block, which t8 (width 8) separates from a per-position ramp; the autotuned kernel configuration for the 16-query verify shape on this card, which ff15 (width 15 with the race skipped) tests directly, and which ff1 on card 1 hinted at by running 15 percent faster than its autotuned twin at width 15; and the fork's split-KV Triton verify attention (VLLM_SPEC_DECODE_ATTN, on by default, with its query maximum raised from 8 to 16 at this width), which sa0_15 turns off for the stock verify attention. The launcher's other coupled settings at this boundary (MAX_SEQS 4, MAX_LEN 57344, the CUDA graph memory budget) remain in every cell and are the residual if all three read 56. Registered before the run: t8 near 56 means a boundary, near 23 with a ramp means per-position cost; ff15 near 23 means a slow autotuned config for this shape on the 4090; sa0_15 near 23 means the split-KV verify kernel is the 2.4x at 16 queries on this card, which would be the maintainer note with the mechanism attached.

The alias the image honours is LOOKUP. Every launcher line cited in this document is the checkout's; the image's equivalents sit about 35 lines earlier (its async rule at line 255, its adaptive-length pin at 247). Async at width 15 costs 9.3 percent of throughput there, the opposite sign from width 7: async padding forces the full block every step, the adaptive length is bypassed, and every step verifies fifteen positions whether the lookup has anything for them or not (positions 7 to 14 accept 133 tokens across the cohort instead of 59, and it still does not pay). So the launcher's coupling of synchronous scheduling to the long block is a saving, not a price, and its comment undersells the reason. Provisional on that box until the KV-matched synchronous arm lands (the async arm needed the raised pin and the synchronous one ran at the old pin). On this box a15on2 with the raised pin is queued; if the 15-wide path is what costs 2.4x here, async at 15 should read worse than 9.3 percent, since it forces sixteen queries on every step.

Correction to the launcher-version paragraph above (2026-09-05, 07:50Z). Both boxes run the same launcher: the fork's merged file (680 lines; the image's copy hashes identically to the file at 6869c80, and the other box's tree carries the same lines at the same numbers). The 800-line file is this checkout's, on the v0.28-validation branch, whose four launcher commits after 6869c80 are the port arc's own build-repair work (among them the change that made VLLM_DFLASH2_LOOKUP the canonical variable with LOOKUP as an alias and guarded the export), never merged into the fork. So there is no launcher difference between the boxes to add to the cross-box list, the reviewer's line citations refer to the branch file, and the fork's file has the unconditional export on both boxes: `export VLLM_DFLASH2_LOOKUP=${LOOKUP:-1}` overwrites the variable everywhere, the `:-` guards the alias, not the export. The other box escaped the trap by setting both names in every cell. Its lookup-off cells are confirmed by the measurement's own geometry (per-position counters at positions 0 to 6 only, none past), and that is the void condition the repeat here adopts: with the lookup off there must be no accepted positions past 6 in the counter unless the drafter itself is being asked for the whole block, in which case positions 7 to 14 carry the drafter's own proposals beyond its trained block at a rate the counter will show, and the log must lack the lookup's line. An environment echo can be overwritten and a log line can be absent; the counter cannot report positions that were never verified. With lk15off void, the 2.4x here is unattributed again, with the lookup and the scheduler both back in play, and the cause cells plus lk15off2 and a15on2 carry the question.

Those are different claims and one pair does not separate them. ## std7, the greedy comparison, and a host rule for wall clocks (2026-09-05, 08:05Z)

**std7** (width 7, standard rejection, the launcher default) is a bit-identical replay of w7 on all 32 rows: tokens per step 3.798, per-position 707, 513, 387, 300, 234, 189, 151 per thousand rounds, the same side of the partition. Its step cost is not usable: 45.1 ms per step steady against w7's 23.3 for the same configuration, with card 0 sampled at 8 lanes, Gen4, 2730 to 2745 MHz throughout. The one thing that differed was card 1 running the fast greedy cell (about 27 ms per step) at the same time; host CPU sat at 11 percent of 32 threads. So two engines active at once slow each other through the WSL2 GPU path, not through cores, and the effect is large when the neighbour is fast. Rule, applied from here: a card-0 wall clock is clean only with card 1 idle or slow, and the rest of the card-0 queue (blk7's twin excepted, it is running now beside the second greedy boot) runs with card 1 idle; the two extra cache-off boots are moved behind it. What this does to earlier numbers: every 23-versus-56 comparison above was measured under similar card-1 load (15-token cells at about 90 ms per step on that card), so the 2.4x ratio stands as measured under like conditions, while the absolutes (23.3 and 56.5) are upper bounds until the trajectory count (width 7) and the cause cells (width 15) give clean ones with card 1 idle. Card 1's own clocks were always measured beside a busy card 0 and are treated as contaminated throughout. [Withdrawn 2026-09-05: the neighbour, lane and contamination attributions in this paragraph did not survive; see the section beginning "First, the one-engine-per-host rule loses its evidence" and the corrections from the second fresh-eyes review at the end of this record.]

**The greedy comparison.** g7r, width 7, temperature 0, 32 rows: the four seeds give identical text per prompt (deterministic within a boot, as required), and all eight prompts differ from the native reference in both hash and length (this box 3781, 3669, 3436, 3717, 2359, 3375, 4253, 3549 bytes against 4208, 4334, 3166, 3988, 2168, 3102, 4236, 3848). With tokenizer, template, generation config, model files and engine configuration matched byte for byte, all-eight-differ on 1024-token greedy generations is what a numerics difference that surfaces once per prompt looks like, since one flipped token changes hash and length. The measurement is the first-divergence token index per prompt, which needs the native completions; requested. The second greedy boot (g7s) then says whether this box's greedy text is stable across boots or the boot state reaches the output at temperature 0.

**Both boxes have boot-level variance.** The native box's first two genuinely cache-off boots (engine line False) differ on 8 of 8 rows in rounds and accepted tokens (prompt 1: 309 rounds and 714 accepted against 338 and 687), a real trajectory difference. The corrected cross-box statement replaces the earlier one: the 3090 native shows one trajectory across eight boots with the cache on and two trajectories across two boots with it off; this box shows variance in both regimes and more with the cache off. Prefix caching suppresses the variance there rather than the variance being absent: with the cache on, eight prefills are computed once and replayed by three seeds, so whatever varies per computation is committed once; with it off, every prefill gets its own draw. That is the footprint model above, and it makes "a bit on one card and not the other" a smaller and more explicable difference in how much each regime exposes. The group-count test stands on both boxes: a binary state predicts at most two trajectory groups however many cache-off boots run; the native box's third cache-off boot and this box's third and fourth (pc0c, pc0d, queued last) decide it.

## Trajectory groups counted, and where the greedy texts part (2026-09-05, 08:15Z)

| configuration | boots | groups | differing rows between groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| card 0, width 15, lookup on, sync, cache on | 5 (std1, std1b, lk15off; std2, c0cc0) | 2 | 10, the same ten for every cross-group pair |
| card 0, width 15, block rejection | 2 (blk1, blk2) | 2 | 4 |
| card 0, width 7, async, cache on | 2 (w7, std7) | 1 | 0 |
| card 1, width 15, lookup on (cc0, coh-flag) | 2 | 2 | 3 |
| card 1, width 15, forced long block, three thresholds | 3 (cc4096, cc8192, cc16384) | 3 | 8, 1, 9 |
| card 1, width 15, force-first-config | 2 | 2 | 26 |
| card 1, width 15, cache off | 2 | 2 | 15 |
| native 3090, width 15, cache on | 8 | 1 | 0 |
| native 3090, width 15, cache off | 3 | 2 | (8 of 8 rows between groups) |

Card 0 at width 15 is a clean single bit: five boots, two groups, and every cross-group pair differs on the same ten rows. The native box agrees: eight cache-on boots in one group, three cache-off boots in two. Card 1 does not fit one bit as stated: three forced-block boots, which force identically at this context length, form three groups, with footprints of 8, 1 and 9 rows, and card 1's footprints run from 1 to 26 rows across configurations where card 0's bit has exactly ten every time. Either the three thresholds are not same-behaviour after all (a threshold value that enters the policy's state somewhere at these context lengths), or a second and rarer source sits on top of the bit on that card (the one-row pair looks like that), or card 1 draws more often than once per boot. The fourth and fifth cache-off boots there (pc0c, pc0d) decide between two groups and more.

**Where the greedy texts part.** Against the native box's prefix-hash ladder (sha256 of the first N bytes), this box's greedy completions match through the first 128 bytes on prompts 0, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 7 and differ by 256; prompt 6 matches through 256 and differs by 512; prompt 1 differs already within the first 32 bytes. So the two boxes generate the same first thirty to sixty tokens on nearly every prompt and then a greedy argmax flips once, after which the continuations part and the lengths drift by hundreds of bytes. Nothing upstream differs (prompt 0 opens identically on both: a think block beginning "The user wants a complete Python module implementing an LRU cache with"); the logits of the two cards differ enough that a top-1 near-tie is hit within the first hundred tokens of every prompt. The exact first-divergence token per prompt follows from the 128-to-256-byte brackets, requested.

**Where the greedy texts part, exactly.** Aligning the native brackets against this box's completions, each prompt parts at one greedy decision between the 19th and 53rd word: "Docstrings" against "Docstring" (prompt 0), "safety tips" against "safety advice" (2), "analyze the code:" against "analyze the code first." (3), "Let me set up a coord" against "**Step 1" (4), "Relations" against "Relations (foreign keys)" (5), "An engaging introduction" against "An engaging title and introduction" (6), "has faced significant delays" against "faced a 2023 tender postponement" (7); prompt 1 parts at the first token, the native box opening an empty think block and this box none, a single structural flip that makes it the least informative prompt rather than the most. Every one is a near-tie between two reasonable continuations, decided the other way on the other card. That is what a numerics difference between the two cards' kernels looks like at temperature 0: the same first thirty to sixty tokens, then the first near-tie goes the other way and the continuations part. It is not a bug and not a box fault; it is the reason two cards do not share trajectories, and it is why cross-box comparisons in this document pair nothing and compare only aggregates with their spreads.

**The boot state does not reach the greedy text.** g7r against g7s, two boots of the same configuration on card 1 at temperature 0: identical text on all 32 rows, hash for hash. So the per-boot state that partitions sampled trajectories on this host (ten rows of thirty-two on card 0) is small enough that it never flips a greedy argmax on this cohort; it flips sampled draws that land near a boundary. Sampled runs see it, deterministic runs do not, which is a useful bound on its size and a reason it went unnoticed.
